Title: Fault-Tolerant Quantum Computation with Adversarial Errors

URL Source: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.16857v1

Published Time: Tue, 18 Aug 2026 02:26:32 GMT

Number of Pages: 132

Markdown Content:
# Fault-Tolerant Quantum Computation with Adversarial Errors ∗

Nikolas P. Breuckmann University of Bristol, UK 

niko.breuckmann@bristol.ac.uk 

Louis Golowich UC Berkeley, USA 

lgolowich@berkeley.edu 

Umesh Vazirani UC Berkeley, USA 

vazirani@eecs.berkeley.edu 

August 18, 2026 

Abstract 

We prove a fault-tolerance theorem for quantum computation against adversarial noise. For every quantum circuit on ¯N logical qudits of depth ¯T , we construct a fault-tolerant circuit on 

N = poly( ¯N ) physical qudits of depth ¯T · ¯N o(1) , which is robust against an adversary who may arbitrarily choose and corrupt an almost-linear number N 1−o(1) of physical qudits at each time step. This robustness significantly improves upon prior fault-tolerance theorems, which assumed corruptions were either local and stochastic, or else only act on a polynomially vanishing fraction of qudits. Our fault-tolerance scheme addresses a key bottleneck towards constructing quantum PCPs via the circuit-to-Hamiltonian mapping of Anshu, Breuckmann, and Nguyen (STOC’24). More fundamentally, our result demonstrates that fault-tolerant quantum computation remains pos-sible under noise models that are global, worst-case, and non-Markovian over the full duration of the computation, directly countering concerns that correlated noise could fundamentally un-dermine quantum fault tolerance. Our construction is based on a new family of subsystem product codes we develop, which have large dimension and distance along with low-weight parity-checks, and which support transversal non-Clifford gates. We show how to perform single-shot fault-tolerant error correction on these codes using a Floquet-like procedure based on the local testability of classical tensor codes. We then obtain a universal fault-tolerance scheme using repeated code switching in a hypercubic qudit architecture. Finally, we recursively compose our scheme with itself to reduce an initially exponential qudit dimension down to a constant. 

> ∗

To appear in 67th IEEE Symposium On Foundations Of Computer Science (FOCS) 2026.

31 Introduction 

The feasibility of quantum computation relies on our ability to control the effects of noise. A major achievement in this regard is the quantum fault-tolerance theorem [ABO97, Kit97, KLZ98] which shows that a quantum device made from faulty components can perform any quantum computation with arbitrarily high fidelity. The theorem relies on a number of assumptions, one of which is that errors are probabilistic and localized. More precisely, the fault-tolerance theorem allows for errors that are correlated in space (between qudits) and time (between execution steps), as long as these correlations decay exponentially. Subsequent work has refined and extended the fault-tolerance theorem in several directions. Al-iferis, Gottesman and Preskill [AGP06] gave a rigorous quantitative treatment. A separate line of work has focused on reducing the resource overhead of fault-tolerant protocols: Gottesman [Got14] showed that constant space overhead suffices assuming good quantum LDPC codes, and Yamasaki and Koashi [YK24] subsequently achieved constant space and polylogarithmic time overhead si-multaneously. Most recently, Nguyen and Pattison [NP25] improved the time overhead to almost logarithmic, matching classical fault tolerance up to sub-polylogarithmic factors, while He, Nguyen and Pattison [HNP25] introduced a composable framework that allows threshold proofs to be built modularly from independently analyzed gadgets. All of these results, however, still operate under the assumption that noise is local and stochastic, with correlations decaying exponentially in space and time. Fault-tolerance is more difficult to achieve when the noise is adversarial . In this model, in each time step an adversary may choose an arbitrary set of qudits of some bounded size, and then corrupt these qudits by applying an arbitrary channel, which may introduce long-range correlated errors. Tan et al. [THL +25] presented a fault-tolerance scheme against such adversarial noise that is based on single-shot code switching, building upon a related scheme of Bomb´ ın [Bom16]. However, for a circuit on N physical qudits, these works can only handle at most O(N 1/3) adversarial errors per time step, which has remained the state-of-the-art. 1

Our main result is a fault-tolerance scheme with significantly improved robustness to adver-sarial noise. Specifically, our scheme protects against adversarial corruptions to an almost-linear 

number N 1−o(1) of physical qudits at every time step. 

Theorem 1.1 (Main result; Informal statement of Theorem 7.1) . For every fixed prime power q

and every ϵ > 0, there exists a fault-tolerance scheme over q-dimensional qudits with the following guarantee: Every quantum circuit using ¯N logical qudits and time (i.e. depth) ¯T is compiled to a fault-tolerant physical circuit using N ≤ ¯N 5+ ϵ physical qudits and time T ≤ ¯T · 2O(

√log ¯N ), which is robust to adversarial corruptions on N/ 2O(

√log ¯N ) qudits per timestep. 2

We prove Theorem 1.1 for quantum circuits using a fixed universal 3 gate set—namely, Paulis, Hadamard, CNOT, Toffoli, and initialization/reset—that also have access to free noiseless clas-sical computation. We emphasize that we allow our circuits to have arbitrary quantum inputs and quantum outputs, which may be chosen after compilation of the physical circuit. In this set-ting, fault-tolerance inherently requires physical quantum circuits, as even an all-powerful classical            

> 1To the best of our knowledge.
> 2Recall that 2 O(√log N)=No(1) grows subpolynomially.
> 3This gate set was shown to be universal over Fqfor every prime q≥5 in [ABO97]. Note that to obtain a universal scheme over qubits, one could simulate each 5-dimensional qudit using three qubits. This modification would change the gate set; we do not pursue it for simplicity.

4computer cannot process quantum information. As such, like many other fault-tolerance theorems (e.g. [Got14, HNP25, THL +25]), we primarily use side classical computation in our decoder. See Section 2.2.4 for details. Theorem 1.1 implies that quantum computation can be made robust against noise patterns that are global, worst-case and non-Markovian over the full duration of the computation. Quanti-tatively, we allow corruptions on N −o(1) -fraction of the physical qudits in each timestep, which is a super-polynomial improvement over the O(N −2/3)-fraction of corrupted qudits allowed by prior constructions. Qualitatively, we emphasize the global nature of the adversarial corruptions we pro-tect against. Because any inverse-polynomial fraction N −α of qudits can be entirely corrupted, we cannot use traditional approaches to fault-tolerance that encode each logical qudit in its own code block (e.g. [Neu56, DO77, Pip85, ABO97]). Indeed, such schemes are vulnerable to adversarial cor-ruptions on 1 / ¯N -fraction 4 of the physical qudits, which can wipe out an entire code block, making fault-tolerance impossible. In contrast, we prove Theorem 1.1 by jointly encoding our qudits in a code of high rate and distance throughout the computation, so that there is never a small code block presenting an easy target for an adversary to corrupt. From a foundational perspective, our strong robustness addresses a concern raised by skeptics of quantum computing, who have argued against its feasibility by pointing out that correlated noise could fundamentally undermine fault tolerance [Lev03, Kal11, Kal16]. The concern is that physical noise need not satisfy the locality and independence assumptions underlying existing fault-tolerance theorems, and that uncontrolled degrees of freedom could place the environment in “devilish states capable of conspiracies which defy imagination” [Lev00]. Our result directly addresses this concern: we show that fault-tolerant quantum computation is possible even if nature conspires against us. An adversary with full knowledge of the circuit and the ability to apply coherent, correlated errors to an almost-linear number of qudits at every time step cannot prevent the computation from succeeding. Furthermore, our result addresses a key bottleneck towards constructing quantum PCPs (qPCPs) via adversarial fault-tolerance. The qPCP conjecture posits the existence of a constant soundness gap δ > 0 along with a family of local Hamiltonians 5 for which it is QMA -hard to determine if the ground energy density is ≤ 0 vs ≥ δ. This conjecture has remained a major open question for which relatively little remains known. In contrast, the classical analogue, namely the cele-brated PCP theorem [AS98, ALM +98], has seen multiple different proofs (see e.g. [Din07, Mei12]), with numerous implications throughout complexity theory and cryptography. However, all of these approaches to PCPs have resisted quantization due to various constraints imposed by quantum mechanics [AAV13]. Anshu, Breuckmann, and Nguyen [ABN24] recently proposed a new approach to constructing quantum PCPs that evades these issues and which is based on fault-tolerance. The idea is to compile a logarithmic-depth QMA -verifier circuit into a fault-tolerant version, which can then be mapped to a local Hamiltonian. [ABN24] gave a circuit-to-Hamiltonian mapping for which they conjectured that low-energy states correspond to noisy executions of the circuit, and proved some results towards this conjecture. The noise here may consist of large adversarial corruptions— precisely the error model against which our fault-tolerance scheme is the first to protect! Our                           

> 4It is helpful to consider the regime where the number of physical qudits N≤¯NO(1) grows at most polynomially in the number of logical qudits ¯N, as is the case in all our results.
> 5We consider Hamiltonians H=Pmi=1 Hiacting on Nqudits with m= poly( N) terms Hi, each of which acts on O(1) of the qudits and has bounded norm ∥Hi∥ ≤ O(1). The ground energy density min |ψ⟩⟨ψ|H|ψ⟩/m simply equals the ground energy divided by the number of terms.

5results hence provide a key ingredient towards quantum PCPs with soundness gap δ ≥ 1/N o(1) ,due to our tolerance of errors on 1 /N o(1) -fraction of the qudits; the main remaining missing piece is the conjecture of [ABN24]. In contrast, the best previously known soundness gap for QMA was 

δ = 1 / poly( N ) from the Feynman–Kitaev construction [KSV02]. In fact, the exact reasoning above carries through for the classical case. In [ABG +26], a classical fault-tolerance theorem against adversarial noise is used to prove a ‘polylog-weaker’ classical PCP theorem with inverse-polylogarithmic soundness. The proof follows precisely the strategy outlined above: a circuit is compiled into a fault-detecting circuit via adversarial fault tolerance and then using the Cook–Levin mapping turned into a constraint satisfaction problem whose soundness gap is inherited from the error threshold of the fault-tolerance scheme. The classical construction achieves fault detection against a N/ polylog( N )-fraction of adversarial errors per layer, which translates directly into a PCP with polylogarithmic query complexity. This provides strong evidence that the quantum analogue of this strategy is a viable path toward the quantum PCP conjecture. As a technical remark, as described above our fault-tolerance scheme in Theorem 1.1 relies on noiseless classical computation, which may be difficult to handle under the circuit-to-Hamiltonian mapping. However, [ABG +26] faced a related challenge in the classical setting, which they overcame by relaxing the fault-tolerance requirement to a fault-detection requirement. That is, because the PCP setting inherently concerns verifying computation, it is sufficient to detect errors, rather than correct them. Such error-detection can be performed without decoding, which as mentioned above is our primary use of noiseless classical computation. Using this idea, we believe our fault-tolerance scheme can be adapted to the PCP setting, though the ultimate required circuit model will depend on the details of any resolution to the conjecture of [ABN24]. 

# 2 Technical Overview 

We now provide a more detailed overview of our fault-tolerance scheme. Like nearly all prior fault-tolerance schemes, we assume our input is encoded in a quantum error-correcting code, and we perform computation on the encoded data to compute the desired encoded output. However, our strong adversarial error model places stringent requirements on the codes that we can use. For instance, as described above, we cannot encode each logical qudit into its own code block, as then the adversary could corrupt an entire such block. Hence we must jointly encode our qudits in a code of large (i.e. almost-linear) distance. But then to perform the desired logical computation (which may apply arbitrary gates in parallel to different logical qudits) with just N o(1) time overhead as in Theorem 1.1, we need to perform: 1. Error-correction in the presence of adversarial noise, which corrects more errors than it in-troduces, and 2. Parallel targeted gates on the logical qudits, with limited error propagation. The first item above can be achieved using good quantum LDPC codes, which have low-weight parity-checks that can be measured by low-depth circuits to perform error-correction. The low circuit depth ensures that only a small number of new errors occur during error-correction. Mean-while, the second item above can be achieved using codes supporting transversal gates , meaning that a constant-depth physical application of the gate induces the logical action of the gate on the encoded message. We will specifically need transversal gates that are non-Clifford, such as Toffoli, as Clifford gates alone cannot achieve universal computation. 6A recent breakthrough line of works culminated in the construction of linear-distance quantum LDPC codes, using a specific “balanced/lifted product” of classical LDPC codes [BE21, PK22a]. Separately, linear-distance codes supporting transversal non-Clifford gates were constructed using algebraic codes such as Reed-Solomon and algebraic-geometry [WHY24, GG25a, NP25]. However, these algebraic codes are inherently non-LDPC, as they possess linear-weight parity-checks. Prior attempts to combine the LDPC and transversal non-Clifford properties have yielded length-N codes with either distance ≤ O(√N ) [BMD07a, BMD07b, Bom15, ZSP +23, SPW24, Zhu25, BDET24, Lin24, GL25], or else check weight ≥ Ω( N 1/3) [GG25b]. These parameters are insufficient to prove Theorem 1.1, which instead would require distance ≥ N 1−o(1) and check weight ≤ N o(1) .The independent and concurrent work [LLL26] recently obtained quantum LDPC codes of nearly linear distance ˜Ω( N ) with a transversal non-Clifford gate. However, the transversal gate is only shown to induce a constant number of logical non-Clifford gates on the encoded qudits, analogously to how the classical repetition code allows for transversal multiplication on a single logical bit. This construction is therefore also insufficient to prove Theorem 1.1, which requires performing (on average) at least a polynomial number of logical non-Clifford gates per timestep. Furthermore, while some level of addressability has been achieved for transversal non-Clifford gates (see e.g. [HVWZ25]), existing constructions still for instance do not support parallel fault-tolerant Toffoli gates on arbitrary triples of logical qudits. Hence known constructions of addressable gates remain insufficient to prove Theorem 1.1. 

2.1 Key Ingredient: New Quantum Subsystem Product Codes 

We overcome these challenges by introducing a new family of quantum codes that simultaneously have high distance and dimension, low check weight, support transversal non-Clifford gates, and are flexible enough to support logical qudit permutations that facilitate parallel targeted gates. As described below, we specifically obtain all of these properties for a new family of subsystem codes over exponentially large alphabets. Error-correction on these subsystem codes requires measuring non-commuting parity-checks (called gauge operators ), which we nevertheless show how to perform in a single-shot fault-tolerant manner by applying classical local testability. Meanwhile, to reduce the alphabet size from exponential down to constant, we recursively compose our fault-tolerance scheme with itself, to simulate high-dimensional qudits using lower-dimensional qudits. 

2.1.1 Reed-Solomon Codes 

We construct our subsystem codes from products of Reed-Solomon codes, building upon the tech-niques of [GG25b]. Recall that classical Reed-Solomon codes consist of evaluations of bounded-degree univariate polynomials over a finite field Fq (see Definition 3.5). Reed-Solomon codes have linear dimension and distance, along with a transversal multiplication property : the component-wise product 6 of two Reed-Solomon codewords lies in another, higher-dimensional Reed-Solomon code. It was shown by [WHY24, GG25a, NP25] that a quantum CSS code constructed from two Reed-Solomon codes exhibits a transversal CCX (i.e. Toffoli) gate, which is non-Clifford. 7 This                   

> 6The component-wise product of a, b ∈Fnqis a∗b= ( a1b1, . . . , a nbn)∈Fnq.
> 7Strictly speaking [WHY24, GG25a, NP25] provided a transversal CCZ gate across three code states. However, we can instead obtain transversal CCX by replacing the third code C= ( CX, C Z) with its dualized version ( CZ, C X), as CCX is equivalent up to CCZ up to conjugation of the third qudit by Hadamard gates.

7result is natural given that the CCX gate, defined by CCX |x1, x 2, x 3⟩ = |x1, x 2, x 3 + x1x2⟩, sim-ply performs multiplication in the standard basis. In fact, a similar idea was used decades ago by [ABO97] to prove the fault-tolerance theorem against local noise. However, the duals of Reed-Solomon codes are themselves Reed-Solomon codes, meaning that the parity-checks have linear weight. [GG25b] proposed a way to reduce the parity-check weight while preserving transversal CCX by taking a subsystem product of Reed-Solomon codes [ZP20], as defined in Definition 2.1 below. 

2.1.2 Subsystem Product Construction with Transversal Non-Clifford Gates 

The subsystem products we use can be viewed as a quantum generalization of classical tensor products. Recall that for classical codes C1, . . . , C u ⊆ Fnq , the tensor product code C = N

Naturally, tensor codes have weight-n parity-checks supported inside these columns. Tensor products of Reed-Solomon codes also retain the transversal multiplication property, which was used by [ABG +26] to construct a classical fault-tolerance scheme. We will similarly use transversal CCX gates on subsystem products of quantum Reed-Solomon codes to obtain our quantum fault-tolerance scheme. 2.1.3 Fault-Tolerant Error-Correction 

Performing error-correction on these subsystem product codes in Definition 2.1 is more involved. In the language of stabilizer subsystem codes, the product code C has weight-n gauge operators

⊆ Fn×nq represents X parity-checks, i.e. gauge operators. Meanwhile, CZ /(CZ ∩ C⊥ 

> X

)represents the space of X logical operators. 9of the tensor code CX or CZ (see above), where the product code C has length nu ≫ n assuming 

u ≫ 1 (we will take u = log n). Hence the gauge-operators are low-weight, and can be measured by low-depth circuits with limited error propagation. However, because C is a subsystem code, these gauge operators do not commute. Thus if we try to correct X-errors by measuring Z-gauge operators, we collapse our state to a Z-gauge eigenstates, which are not X-gauge eigenstates. We can then try to correct Z-errors by measuring X-gauge operators, but will collapse the state to X-gauge eigenstates, which are not Z-gauge eigenstates. Alternating between measuring low-weight X and Z gauge operators in this way, we obtain a dynamic error-correction protocol reminiscent of floquet codes [HH21]. In particular, after measuring Z-gauge operators, we apply a correction to move the code state into a simultaneous +1-eigenspace of all Z-gauge operators. It is on this gauge-fixed code state that we are able to apply transversal CCX (see Section 5.7 for details). However, we must perform the error-correction described above in the presence of measurement noise. For non-subsystem LDPC codes, commuting measurements can be repeated to identify measurement errors (see e.g. [Got14]). In contrast, errors in the non-commuting measurements of subsystem codes are more difficult to handle, as repeating the measurements will collapse the state into different eigenspaces at each step. Nevertheless, we are able to perform fault-tolerant error-correction on subsystem product codes using a single-shot strategy suggested in [GG25b, Appendix C]. Specifically, the noisy gauge oper-ator measurements in a given basis ( X or Z) give a noisy syndrome s consisting of parity-checks for the corresponding tensor code ( CX or CZ ). We then find the s′ that is closest to s in Hamming weight such that s′ is the true syndrome of some error e′ (i.e. s′ would be a valid measurement in the absence of noise), and we decode assuming s′ was our syndrome. This procedure successfully corrects errors because classical tensor codes are locally testable [Vid15], meaning that the error e′

inducing syndrome s′ must be close to the true error on the code state, as long as s′ is close to the true error’s syndrome. See Section 5.1 for more details. 

2.1.4 Distance Analysis 

We described above how to perform fault-tolerant error-correction and transversal CCX gates on subsystem products of Reed-Solomon codes. It remains to be shown that we can construct such codes of large (i.e. almost-linear) distance. For factor codes C1, . . . , C u of respective distances 

d1, . . . , d u, the quantum subsystem product C in general may have distance D between max i∈[u] di

and Q 

> i∈[u]

di. To obtain almost-linear distance D ≥ N 1−o(1) with respect to the block length N

of C, we want D ≈ Q 

> i∈[u]

di to roughly match the upper bound, assuming each di grows linearly in the length of Ci. Note that classical tensor product codes, in contrast, always have distance 

D = Q 

> i∈[u]

di.Closing the gap between these lower and upper distance bounds in the quantum case has proven to be challenging. Several works have studied this problem for various families of product codes [BH14, AC18, ZP20, GG25c, GG25b], though only the latter paper also required transversal non-Clifford gates. Specifically, [GG25b] constructed u = 2 carefully chosen Reed-Solomon codes, as well as u = 3 randomly punctured product Reed-Solomon codes over exponentially large alphabets, for which the subsystem product has distance D ≥ N 1−o(1) and supports transversal non-Clifford gates. Their proof relied on a strong “product-expansion” property that is difficult to achieve for algebraic codes, and did not extend to u > 3. Hence the parity-check weight was ≥ N 1/3,10 which means that syndrome extraction requires a polynomial-depth circuit, and hence errors on a polynomially small fraction of qudits may lead to an entirely corrupted syndrome. [GG25b] left it as an open question to extend these results to u > 3. We resolve this question by constructing a family of tuples of u quantum Reed-Solomon codes for which we show the subsystem product has distance D ≥ N 1−o(1) , where u → ∞ as N → ∞ .We present our codes using the following notation for polynomial evaluations.

2.1.5 Aside: Non-Subsystem Product Codes and Further Implications 

We believe that our codes are of independent interest, beyond the context of our fault-tolerance scheme. Indeed, the construction of quantum codes of almost-linear distance and dimension with low (e.g. subpolynomial) check weight was itself a longstanding open question, which was resolved by the breakthrough line of works [HHO21, PK22b, BE21, PK22a] leading to asymptotically good quantum LDPC codes, which have linear distance and dimension and constant check weight, over constant-sized alphabets. All constructions to date of such codes [BE21, PK22a, LZ22, DHLV23, LH22, HLM +25] consist of similar ingredients, namely the balanced product [BE21] (or the closely related lifted product [PK22b]) of classical codes, combined with a local robustness property called “product-expansion” [PK22a, KP23]. Even neglecting the transversal non-Clifford property (which was not obtained by the prior works above), our codes can be viewed as a contribution to this line of works with a fundamen-tally different construction and a simple, self-contained analysis. Just as the good qLDPC codes described above are non-subsystem codes, in Section A we also give a non-subsystem version of our codes, which lose the transversal non-Clifford property, but still retain almost-linear distance and 12 dimension with subpolynomial check weight over exponentially large alphabets. While a related construction was given in [GG25b] that applied the high-dimensional product-expansion result of [KP25] to random product codes, our construction is explicit, with a concise and self-contained analysis as described in Section 2.1.4 above. 

2.2 Fault-Tolerance Scheme 

We now describe how we apply our polynomial evaluation subsystem product codes from Section 2.1 to construct our fault-tolerance scheme in Theorem 1.1. Our key idea here is to combine transversal gates on our subsystem product codes C = N 

> i∈[u]

Ci with repeated code switching. In this code switching, for each i ∈ [u], we un-encode the factor code Ci, and then re-encode into some ( Ci)′

that may be different from Ci.This code switching performs two purposes. First, it allows us to obtain a universal set of transversal gates, which according to the Eastin-Knill theorem must require some sort of code switching (see the discussion of transversal Hadamard below). Second, because we can apply transversal gates after un-encoding but before re-encoding, we are able to perform gates between any two qudits that lie in the same direction direction-i column, for any i. Hence we are able to perform circuits with a u-dimensional hypercubic connectivity, which enables efficient logical qudit permutations, and hence targeted parallel gates. We provide more details below. Related code switching techniques have been used in prior quantum fault-tolerance schemes, e.g. [Bom16, JO19, THL +25, GCZ25, XZB +25]. The classical fault-tolerance scheme of [Spi96] also uses similar ideas, and like our scheme is based on tensor products of Reed-Solomon codes. However, such schemes typically only used products of a constant number of factor codes, which leads to a smaller polynomial error tolerance. Our use of code switching is most similar to the classical adversarial fault-tolerance scheme of [ABG +26], which also used a growing number of factor codes. 

2.2.1 Transversal Gates 

Recall from Section 2.1.2 that our codes support transversal CCX gates. As (even subsystem) CSS codes, they naturally also support Pauli gates (Lemma 5.13), CX gates (Lemma 5.14), and Hadamard gates (Lemma 5.11). In each case, the logical gate is induced by applying the physical gate across code states, which as a depth-1 circuit cannot propagate errors within a code state. For the Hadamard gate, the transversal application maps a code state of C = ( CX , C Z ) to a code state of the dualized code C⊥ = ( CZ , C X ). This dualized code is still a polynomial evaluation subsystem product code by the self-duality formula in Equation (1) in Section 2.1.4 above, so it still has large distance by Theorem 2.4. We can then return to the original code by switching out the dualized factor codes for the original Ci one at a time, as described below. 

2.2.2 Code Switching 

As described above, to perform code switching on a subsystem product code C = N 

> i∈[u]

Ci, we simply un-encode one factor Ci, and then re-encode, possibly into a different code ( Ci)′. We subsequently perform error-correction, and then can repeat in the other directions, to sequentially switch all u factor codes. 13 Recall that we take u = log n where n is the length of each factor code Ci, so that C has length 

N = nu = nlog n. Each switching operation simply applies a depth-poly( n) un-encoding and then re-encoding circuit within each direction-i column. Therefore each error on a qudit can only propagate to the n qudits in the same direction-i column. Hence we can withstand N/ poly( n) = N 1−o(1) 

errors per time step of this switching procedure without overloading the code’s distance, which is also N/ poly( n) (see Theorem 2.4). 

2.2.3 Universal Fault-Tolerant Computation in Hypercubic Architecture 

We now describe how we combine the code switching and transversal gates described above to obtain our scheme for universal fault-tolerant computation. Let each factor code Ci have dimension k,so that C has logical qudits labeled by [ k]u. After un-encoding Ci but before re-encoding, our ku

logical qudits are distributed across k code states of N 

> i′∈[u]\{ i}

Ci′

, each containing ku−1 logical qudits. We can perform the transversal gates described above across these k code states, before switching back up to the code C.We therefore want to compile our logical circuit into one consisting of a sequence of transversal gates, each acting along a single direction i ∈ [u] of the u-dimensional hypercube [ k]u of logical qudits. For this purpose, we apply an identical idea as in the classical case [ABG +26], which also appeared in prior works such as [Spi96]: using bitonic sorting networks [Bat68], we can implement arbitrary permutations of qudits on the hypercube via short sequences of transversal gates. Hence we can route qudits into arbitrary positions, apply arbitrary desired transversal gates, and then route qudits back to their original positions. By routing qudits to appropriate positions with respect to the subsequent transversal gates, we can perform arbitrary targeted gates in parallel. It follows from [Bat68] that this compilation increases the circuit depth by a factor of at most O(u2 log 2 k). Note that the geometry aids fault-tolerance in multiple ways. First, even after the downwards switching step we are still protected by an almost linear distance when u = log n, i.e. we are always protected by the bulk. Second, as gates are applied in a single direction, errors spread only within columns. Every fault-tolerant gadget in our scheme follows a common pattern: we first run error-correction (Lemma 5.2) to reduce the error weight, then apply the desired gate gadget, and finally run error-correction again to clean up any new errors introduced by the gate and any faults that occurred during its execution. We choose parameters to ensure that the number of errors always remains at most N/ poly( n) = N 1−o(1) .

2.2.4 Fault-Tolerance Formalism 

The fault-tolerance notation and definitions we use are adapted from the composable gadget for-malism of Nguyen and Pattison [NP25] and He, Nguyen, and Pattison [HNP25], and most closely follows the subsequent presentation in [GCZ25]. We model a fault-tolerant gadget (Definition 3.29) as a physical circuit Q that implements a logical operation ¯O on states encoded into quantum error-correcting codes, provided that the fault, an arbitrary sequence of superoperators applied after each timestep, avoids certain “bad sets” Erun specifying the maximum tolerable error weight per timestep. The input and output codes are each decorated with an encoding map Enc, a set Γ of allowed gauge qudit states, and a family E of bad sets defining the maximum tolerable error on the encoded state. A gadget is fault-tolerant if, whenever the input state is a low-weight deviation 14 from a valid codeword and the fault avoids the bad sets, the output is again a low-weight deviation from the correctly encoded logical output. Gadgets can be composed sequentially (Lemma 3.33) as long as each gadget’s output error avoids all bad sets of the subsequent gadget’s decorated input code. Parallel composition (Lemma 3.34) is handled by including an external system of ℓ auxiliary qudits into the definition that may be entangled with the encoded state, ensuring that fault-tolerance guarantees are preserved under tensor products with other components of a larger computation. We discuss the more involved notion of simulative composition below, which we use to reduce the qudit dimension of our scheme. While our fault-tolerance definitions involve Pauli errors and Pauli faults, they in fact capture protection against fully general quantum noise. By the Pauli decomposition (Lemma 3.17), every superoperator can be expressed as a linear combination of Pauli superoperators with supports contained in the support of the original superoperator. This allows us to reduce the analysis of arbitrary quantum channels, including coherent errors, to Pauli errors (Lemma 3.30). Furthermore, Lemma 3.32 shows that our formalism extends to an extended fault model in which the adversary maintains a private register of ancilla qudits that can be entangled with the system throughout the computation, capturing the strongest possible noise model. Similar ideas were described in [HNP25], which we adapt to our framework. Our gate set G = {H∗, X∗, Z∗, CX ∗, CCX ∗, Init ∗, CF ∗} includes, besides the familiar standard gates, classical function gates CF ∗ (Definition 3.13), which perform a basis measurement followed by a noiseless classical computation and reinitialization. 8 We use these gates to decode the noisy syndromes described in Section 2.1.3, as well as to help handle Hadamard gates in the simulative composition described in Section 2.2.5 below for alphabet reduction. Because we prove fault-tolerance even against errors induced by non-physical (i.e. non-channel) superoperators, an error following a classical function gate may induce a postselection on our logical state. For instance, suppose we apply a classical function gate that measures a quantum code state in the standard basis, and outputs the measurement outcome of some logical qudit. A single-qudit error superoperator could then postselect on any given measurement outcome that has positive probability. As a result, our final fault-tolerance result (Theorem 7.1) only guarantees that the fault-tolerant circuit performs the desired logical computation under some postselected versions of the logical classical function gates. Fortunately, logical circuits consisting of unitary gates and single-qudit mid-circuit measure-ments (i.e. the ordinary quantum circuit model) never need to perform classical function gates, and hence avoid postselection entirely. Specifically, unitary gates never involve postselection. Mean-while, a mid-circuit standard-basis measurement can be performed by applying CX to couple the data qudit with an ancilla qudit initialized to |0⟩, and then re-initializing (to effectively trace out) the ancilla qudit. Crucially, our initialization gadget (Lemma 5.4) does not induce any logical postselection, so we can fault-tolerantly perform such mid-circuit measurements without postselec-tion. Thus, despite the presence of postselections in our formalism, we can fault-tolerantly compile non-unitary logical circuits without any logical postselection. 

2.2.5 Alphabet Reduction via Simulative Composition 

The fault-tolerance scheme described above operates over qudits of dimension q′ that grows ex-ponentially in the other parameters, due to the large alphabet required in our distance analysis 

> 8While these gates allow noiseless queries to classical functions, the basis measurement ensures that the queries cannot be made in superposition, so they perform truly classical computations.

15 in Theorem 2.4. To obtain a result over qudits of any fixed prime power dimension r we apply 

simulative composition in Section 7. We first show how a quantum circuit over a small field Fr can be simulated by a circuit over a large extension field Fq′ via the natural inclusion map Fr ,→ Fq′ , by embedding each r-dimensional qudit into a q′-dimensional qudit, see Section 7.2. For most gates in our gate set, the simulation is straightforward: Pauli gates X a, Z a, controlled gates CX a, CCX a, and initialization gates Init ∗ all lift naturally from Fr to Fq′ . The main subtlety is implementing the Hadamard gate H over Fr using operations over Fq′ . Our approach (Lemma 7.2) proceeds as follows: we first apply H over Fq′ , then use the Frobenius map z 7 → zr to compute zr − z, whose kernel is precisely Fr ⊆ Fq′ . By measuring 

zr − z and applying a correction derived from the finite field trace tr Fq′ /Fr (and computed using a classical function gate), we project the output back into Fr, effectively implementing H over the base field. The resulting simulation increases the circuit depth by only an O(log r) factor. We are then able to apply our large-alphabet fault-tolerance scheme described above (see Sec-tion 6 for the details) to this compiled logical circuit over Fq′ , as long as q′ is exponentially large in the number of logical qudits ¯N . Specifically, we take q′ = r2v′

for v′ ≈ log ¯N .We subsequently show in Section 7.3 how to reduce the physical qudit dimension from q′ back towards r by recursively composing our large-alphabet fault-tolerance scheme with smaller instances of itself. The key technical tool is Corollary 7.18, which shows that given a fault-tolerant gadget over 

q′-dimensional qudits with q′ = r2v′

, one can construct a fault-tolerant gadget over q-dimensional qudits with q = r2v

for v = ⌊(3 /4 + ϵ)v′⌋, by encoding each q′-dimensional qudit into a block of 

q-dimensional qudits using the codes from Section 4. We fault-tolerantly simulate each gate on a q′-dimensional qudit using a smaller instance of our fault-tolerance scheme over q-dimensional qudits from Section 6. We then recursively repeat this procedure to obtain a sequence of fault-tolerant circuits over successively smaller alphabets q = r2v

.Crucially, each such composition step preserves the fault-tolerance properties up to controlled losses: the code distance is multiplied by a factor of 2 v−O(√v), the error threshold per time step is multiplied by 2 v−O(√v), and the circuit depth increases by only a 2 O(√v) factor. We choose ϵ < 1/8so that each application strictly reduces the exponent v, and after m = O(log v′) iterations, the exponent drops below a constant v0(ϵ) that depends only on ϵ. At this point, the qudit dimension ˜q = r2v0(ϵ)

is a constant (depending on r and ϵ). Our recursive simulative composition described above stops working at such small qudit dimension, so we instead apply a final, simpler alphabet reduction step (Lemma 7.19) that replaces each ˜ q-dimensional qudit with 2 v0(ϵ) r-dimensional qudits via the field isomorphism F˜q ∼= F2v0(ϵ) 

> r

; this step incurs only a constant-factor overhead since ˜ q is bounded. The cumulative effect of all composition steps is controlled by a geometric series with ratio 3/4 + ϵ and starting value v′ ≈ log ¯N . That is, let v(1) , . . . , v (m) be the sequence of values of v

across all steps. Then the total block length overhead is roughly 2 Pmi=1 v(i)

≤ 2v′/(1 /4−ϵ) = ¯N 4+ O(ϵ),which is polynomial in ¯N , while the total depth overhead is 2 O(Pmi=1  

> √v(i))

= 2 O(√log N ), which is subpolynomial in ¯N . Similarly, the distance and error threshold degrade by at most subpolynomial factors of the form 2 −O(√log N ) relative to the block length. Observe that our final space overhead ¯N 4+ O(ϵ) was determined by the ratio 3 /4 + ϵ of the geometric series above. Ultimately, this ratio is bottlenecked by the alphabet requirement in Theo-rem 2.4, along with a space-time tradeoff we use to efficiently apply linear transformations over the small field, which are needed to simulate CX, CCX, and H gates over the large field. Specifically, 16 our codes in Theorem 2.4 can have alphabet size roughly exponential in the number of logical qudits. Therefore we could hope to set each v(i+1) ≈ v(i)/2, so that each r2v(i)

-dimensional qudit can be encoded in the ≈ 2v(i+1) 

logical qudits in an instance of our code over alphabet size r2v(i+1) 

.However, we instead set v(i+1) ≈ 3v(i)/4 to provide extra ancilla logical qudits at every level of composition. We use these ancilla qudits to apply linear transformations to our logical data qudits in logarithmic depth but with polynomial space overhead. This space-time tradeoff is important for maintaining low circuit depth at every level of concatenation, which ensures errors do not have time to accumulate during the recursive simulations. We leave it as an open question to reduce this space overhead (see Section 2.3). 

2.2.6 Proof of Main Result: Putting it all Together 

Combining the above ideas yields our main result Theorem 1.1. To summarize, fix any prime power r

and any 0 < ϵ < 1/8. Given a logical circuit ¯Q on ¯N r -dimensional qudits of depth ¯T using the universal gate set G = {H∗, X∗, Z∗, CX ∗, CCX ∗, Init ∗, CF ∗}, we construct a fault-tolerant physical circuit Q over r-dimensional qudits as follows. First, we embed the logical circuit into a circuit over a large extension field Fq′ via the inclusion Fr ,→ Fq′ using Lemma 7.2, increasing the depth by a factor of O(log r). We then apply Theorem 6.1 to compile this large-alphabet circuit into a fault-tolerant circuit over Fq′ -qudits using subsystem product codes, where the hypercubic architecture and direction-by-direction code switching provide mending fault-tolerant gadgets for every gate in the universal set. Finally, the recursive alphabet reduction of Section 7.3 brings the qudit dimension back down to r through O(log log ¯N ) rounds of simulative composition (Corollary 7.18), followed by a single application of direct alphabet reduction (Lemma 7.19). The resulting physical circuit Q uses N ≤ ¯N 5+ O(ϵ), and has depth T ≤ 2O(

√log ¯N ) · ¯T . The input and output logical states are encoded into CSS non-subsystem codes obtained by concatenating the polynomial evaluation subsystem product codes of Theorem 4.2 through the recursive composition, yielding code distance d ≥ 2−O(

√log ¯N ) ·N . The fault-tolerant circuit guarantees correctness against any adversarial fault pattern affecting fewer than 2 −O(

√log ¯N ) · N = N 1−o(1) physical qudits per time step, where the o(1) term vanishes as ¯N → ∞ for fixed r and ϵ.By Lemma 3.32, the adversary may apply arbitrary quantum channels, including coherent errors, at each time step, and may furthermore maintain a private register of ancilla qudits that can be entangled with the system throughout the computation. This captures the strongest possible noise model: the adversary has full knowledge of the circuit, chooses errors adaptively, and the errors need not be Markovian or local in any sense. Despite this, the computation succeeds provided only that the total number of affected qudits per step is sublinear. 

2.3 Future Directions 

Several directions for future work remain. First, it may be possible to improve the space overhead of our construction from polynomial to almost linear by encoding many small-alphabet qudits into each large-alphabet qudit during the simulative composition step, similarly to techniques in [GG25a, Ngu25]. The main obstacles would be reducing the space overhead of the linear transformations used to implement the CX, CCX, and Hadamard gates in Lemma 7.4, as well as routing the small qudits within each large qudit. Alternatively, a larger goal would be to directly reduce the alphabet size of our codes in Theorem 2.4. 17 Second, it is natural to ask whether the N o(1) time overhead can be reduced, for instance to poly log N , or whether the error threshold can be improved. Such improvements could ultimately translate to quantum PCPs with larger soundness gaps. Third, our construction does not address the question of decoder efficiency, as we assume the classical decoding problem is performed instantly by classical function gates. It remains unclear whether there exist efficient decoders for quantum product codes in which all factors are quantum codes or chain complexes of dimension ≥ 2. [GG25b, Sections 5–6] provided some progress in this direction, by showing how to decode the product of a pair of quantum Reed-Solomon codes, though instantiated in a different parameter regime from ours. Finally, our scheme may be naturally adaptable to run with nearest-neighbor interactions in 

u spatial dimensions, but since we take u growing with N , this yields a tradeoff between spatial locality and error threshold for any fixed number of spatial dimensions. It would be interesting to understand whether there are “dynamic BPT bounds” that establish fundamental limitations on such tradeoffs, analogously to the bounds of [BPT10] that limit (static) code parameters in a bounded number of spatial dimensions. 

2.4 Roadmap 

In Section 3 we fix notation and provide the technical foundation and the construction of our fault-tolerance framework. In Section 4, we introduce our primary coding-theoretic contribution: a family of quantum subsystem codes derived from tensor products of Reed–Solomon codes. In Section 5.1, we show how the classical local testability of these products enables single-shot error correction, which is essential for maintaining stability against adversarial noise without repeated measurements. We give the construction of a set of universal gates for these codes in the remainder of Section 5. The architectural heart of our protocol is detailed in Section 6, where we describe the hypercubic arrangement of qudits and formalize our code-switching mechanism. This approach allows us to perform transversal operations and qudit routing along specific dimensions of the hypercube while maintaining the protection of the code’s global distance. The main result of this section is an adversarial fault-tolerance theorem where the local qudit dimension grows exponentially in the system size. Finally, Section 7 describes our alphabet reduction techniques. Specifically, we show how to simulate low-dimensional qudits using high-dimensional qudits in Section 7.2, and then we show how to recursively reduce the qudit dimension via simulative composition in Section 7.3. We combine these components in our main result, Theorem 7.1, which states the formal fault-tolerance theorem for quantum computation under adversarial noise. 

# 3 Preliminaries 

3.1 Notation 

For n ∈ N, we write [ n] = {1, 2, . . . , n }. For vectors x, y ∈ Fn over an arbitrary field F, we write 

x ∝ y if there exists some α ∈ F such that x = αy . For a prime power q = pe, we let Fq denote the finite field of order q, and we let F∗ 

> q

= ( Fq \ { 0}). For a subfield Fq′ ⊆ Fq, we let tr Fq /Fq′ : Fq → Fq′

3.2 Error-Correcting Codes 

In this section we present definitions and basic results regarding the error correcting codes used in our fault-tolerance construction. In particular, we make use of quantum codes constructed from tensor products of classical codes. The tensor product structure is crucial for our fault-tolerance protocol. One property in particular is that tensor products of classical codes are locally testable [Vid15], which allows for the construction of efficient fault-tolerant error correction gadgets. 

Definition 3.1. A classical (linear) code of length n and dimension k over Fq is a k-dimensional linear subspace C ⊆ Fnq . The distance of C is d := min c∈C\{ 0} |c|. We summarize these parameters by saying that C is a [n, k, d ]q code. The dual of C is C⊥ := {x ∈ Fnq : x · c = 0 ∀c ∈ C}.For codes C ⊆ Fnq , C′ ⊆ Fn′ 

> q

, the tensor product is the code C ⊗ C′ ⊆ Fn×n′ 

> q

given by C ⊗ C′ =span {c ⊗ c′ : c ∈ C, c ′ ∈ C′}. Equivalently, C ⊗ C′ contains all n × n′ matrices such that every column lies in C and every row lies in C′.

We often refer to classical linear codes as simply “linear codes,” as we will not consider nonlinear codes in this paper. We will use the result of [Vid15] that high-dimensional tensor product codes are locally testable ,as stated below. Our specific presentation of this result of [Vid15] below mirrors that in [ABG +26], though we repeat the details for the reader’s convenience. 

Definition 3.2. For u ∈ N, for i ∈ [u] let Hi ∈ Fmi×ni

be a matrix. Define sets 

N = [ n1] × · · · × [nu]

and S = F

The product-code parity-check matrix H ∈ FS×Nq associated to H1, . . . , H u is given for x ∈ FNq by 

Hx = (( I⊗i−1 ⊗ Hi ⊗ I⊗u−i)x)i∈[u] ∈ M

= FSq .

Proposition 3.3 (Local testability of tensor product codes [Vid15]) . For u, n, d ∈ N, for i ∈ [u]

let Ci = ker( Hi) be an [n, k i, d i ≥ d]q code with full-rank parity-check matrix Hi ∈ F(n−ki)×nq . Let 

H ∈ FS×[N ] 

> q

be the associated product-code parity-check matrix. Then for every s ∈ im( H), there exists some x ∈ FNq with Hx = s and |x| ≤ μ(u, n, d ) · | s|, where 

μ(u, n, d ) = 



un ·

 nd

uη

for an absolute constant η > 0.

The parameter μ(u, n, d ) is called the filling constant of the tensor product code N 

> i∈[u]

Ci and the inverse 1 /μ (u, n, d ) is called the soundness .

Remark 3.4 . We have stated Proposition 3.3 slightly differently from [Vid15, Theorem 3.1], but our statement still follows directly from [Vid15]. Specifically: 1. [Vid15, Theorem 3.1] is stated assuming all codes Ci are equal, though as noted in [Vid15, Remark 4.5], the techniques extend flawlessly to different Ci.2. The tester in [Vid15, Theorem 3.1] actually checks if any of the n − ki entries of s in a given direction-i column are nonzero, meaning that |s| in Proposition 3.3 would be replaced by the number of such direction-i columns with a nonzero entry. Yet this quantity is always within a factor of n of |s|, so we can absorb the factor of n into the nη factor in μ(u, n, d ). 3. When u is not a power of 3, the local tester in [Vid15] samples different syndrome components with different probabilities, meaning that it may seem that we should add different weights for different components when computing the Hamming norm |s| in Proposition 3.3. However, the result of [Vid15] naturally holds under all different permutations of the codes C1, . . . , C u,and all permutations of the n columns and the n − ki rows in each Hi. Averaging over all of these permutations, we conclude that the result holds with uniform weights on the components of the syndrome s.The primary classical codes we consider are polynomial evaluation codes, defined below.

3.3 Quantum Circuits and Fault-Tolerance 

In this section, we provide basic definitions regarding quantum circuits and fault-tolerance. Our definitions are inspired by those in [NP25, HNP25], and most closely resemble those in the sub-sequent work [GCZ25]. The main contribution of this section is Definition 3.29, which formally defines what it means for a logical operation to be fault-tolerant. 

3.3.1 Gates 

In this section, we present the gates we will use in this paper. 

Definition 3.12. A gate acting on m q -dimensional qudits is a quantum channel G : CFmq ×Fmq →

CFmq ×Fmq . A unitary gate is a gate of the form G(ρ) = U ρU † for some unitary U ∈ CFmq ×Fmq .

We now present the gate set that we will use. Here (and throughout the paper) we often refer to unitary gates G(ρ) = U ρU † by the unitary U , as opposed to the channel G.

24 Remark 3.15 . We use the classical function gates CF X,f and CF Z,f in Definition 3.13 to cap-ture the notion of noiseless classical side-computation within an otherwise noisy quantum cir-cuit. Specifically, they perform respectively either X- or Z-basis measurements on some number

> q

The ζ-postselected versions CF ζX,f , CF ζZ,f similarly perform measurements and then apply f ,but they additionally apply postselection to the measurement outcome according to the amplitudes given by ζ.We emphasize that because gates in CF ∗ perform X- or Z-basis measurements, the classical function f cannot be queried in superposition. This definition is in contrast to oracles often studied in quantum complexity theory that can be queried in superposition. We will use the following standard notion of multi-qudit Pauli operators, which are simply defined as tensor products of single-qudit Pauli gates: 

Definition 3.16. An n-qudit Pauli operator Fq is an operator Xa Zb := N

and P ′.

Lemma 3.17 below shows the well-known result that every operator and superoperator has a unique decomposition into a linear combination of Paulis. We include a proof for completeness, as this lemma is often only stated over F2.

Lemma 3.17 (Well known) . Every operator A ∈ CFnq ×Fnq (resp. superoperator A : CFnq ×Fnq →

CFnq ×Fnq ) has a unique decomposition A = P 

> P

αP P as a linear combination of n-qudit Pauli op-erators P (resp. Pauli superoperators P ), for some coefficients αP ∈ C. Furthermore, supp( A) = S   

> P:αP̸=0

supp( P ).Proof. There are q2n = dim C(CFnq ×Fnq ) Pauli operators, and they are all orthogonal with respect to the inner product ( A, B ) 7 → tr  AB †. Thus Pauli operators form a basis for CFnq ×Fnq .Similarly, viewing superoperators as operators on 2 n qudits via the isomorphism {A : CFnq ×Fnq →

CFnq ×Fnq } ∼= CF2nq ×F2nq , then we apply the same argument as above. That is, there are q4n =dim C(CF2nq ×F2nq ) Pauli superoperators, and they are all orthogonal with respect to the inner prod-uct ( A, B ) 7 → tr  AB †; here AB † refers to the composition of A and B† as superoperators, or equivalently to their product as operators in CF2nq ×F2nq . Thus Pauli superoperators form a basis for the space of all superoperators. For both operators and superoperators, the claim that supp( A) = S   

> P:αP̸=0

supp( P ) then follows from the fact that every n-qudit Pauli is a tensor product of n single-qudit Paulis, and the identity is one of the single-qudit Paulis. Specifically, for a given qudit i ∈ [n], if every P with αP̸ = 0 has the identity factor at position I, then we can factor out this identity to write A = A[n]\{ i} ⊗ I{i}, so that i / ∈ supp( A). Conversely, if i / ∈ supp( A), then by definition we can write A = A[n]\{ i} ⊗ I{i},and hence every P with αP̸ = 0 has the identity factor at position I. Thus indeed i ∈ supp( A) iff 

i ∈ supp( P ) for some P with αP̸ = 0. 25 3.3.2 Circuits 

In this section, we define quantum circuits and faults. 

Definition 3.18. A quantum circuit Q = ( Q1, . . . , Q T ; Nin , N out ) acting on a set N of q-dimensional qudits is a sequence Q1, . . . , Q T of superoperators acting on qudits N , along with specified subsets 

Nin , N out ⊆ N . We require that Q1 applies Init X or Init Z to every qudit in N \ Nin .11 We let 

Q(·) : CFNin   

> q×FNin
> q

→ CFNout   

> q×FNout
> q

Q(ρ) = tr N \Nout 

 QT ◦ · · · ◦ Q1

 ρ ⊗ ρ′ ,

where ρ′ ∈ CFN \Nin    

> q×FN\Nin
> q

is a density operator, whose value does not affect Q(ρ) by the definition of Q1. We say Q uses a gate set G if each Qt can be decomposed as a tensor product of gates in G

acting on disjoint sets of q-dimensional qudits. We also say Q uses time T , and when q is clear from context we say that Q uses space N (or as a shorthand, space |N |). A fault on the circuit Q is a sequence F = ( F1, . . . , F T ) of superoperators Ft acting on qudits N .We say F is a Pauli fault if each Ft is a Pauli superoperator, that is, Ft(ρ) = FtρF ′ 

> t

for some Paulis Ft, F ′ 

> t

acting on qudits N . The support supp( F) ⊆ N × [T ] is defined by supp( F) = supp( F1) ⊔ · · · ⊔ supp( FT ).The F-corrupted circuit Q[F] is defined by 

Q[F] = ( Q1, F 1, Q 2, F 2, . . . , Q T , F T ; Nin , N out ).

Let nCF denote the number of CF ∗ gates in Q, and let the ith such gate CF αi,f i act on mi qudits. Let M = F 

> i∈[nCF ]

Fmi 

> q

. Then a postselection on the circuit Q is a vector ζ = ( ζ1, . . . , ζ nCF ) ∈ CM .The ζ-postselected circuit Q[ζ] is defined by replacing the ith CF ∗ gate in Q with its ζi-postselected version CF ζi 

> αi,f i

for every i ∈ [nCF ], and otherwise leaving Q unchanged. It will always be clear from context whether an argument to Q[·] is a fault or postselection. When both are present, we apply the postselection and then the fault, so that Q[F, ζ ] = ( Q[ζ])[ F].We also let Q[∗] = {Q [ζ] : ζ ∈ CM } denote the set of all postselected versions of Q.

We will typically consider quantum circuits using gate set G = {H∗, X∗, Z∗, CX ∗, CCX ∗, Init ∗, CF ∗}.Specifically, the following result from [ABO97] shows that the gates {H∗, X∗, Z∗, CX ∗, CCX ∗, Init ∗}

are sufficient to achieve universal quantum computation over appropriate fields. We will use the gates CF ∗ for decoding in our error-correction gadget (see Section 5.1), as well as for simulating Hadamard gates over some Fq using qudits over another Fq′ (see Section 7.2). 

Lemma 3.19 ([ABO97]) . For every prime q ≥ 5, the gate set G = {H∗, X∗, Z∗, CX ∗, CCX ∗, Init ∗}

over q-dimensional qudits is universal . That is, for every unitary U ∈ CFnq ×Fnq and every ϵ > 0,there exists a circuit Q using gate set G with n input and output qudits such that for every density operator ρ ∈ CFnq ×Fnq , then 

∥Q (ρ) − U ρU †∥ ≤ ϵ. (5) 

Furthermore, Q only needs to call Init ∗ in the first timestep.

In particular, when describing a gadget, we will sometimes specify the initialization gate Init αat a timestep t > 1 for qudits in N\Nin that are not used until time t.In such cases, it should be implicitly assumed that Init αis also applied in timestep 1. For circuits where we do explicitly state that Q1need not apply Init Xor Init Zto every qudit in N\Nin , we will ensure that Qis constructed so that the output Q(ρ) still does not depend on the initial state ρ′of the qudits in

Also note that [ABO97] prove universality for a slightly different gate set, which includes the swap gate |x1⟩ | x2⟩ 7 → | x2⟩ | x1⟩ and the scalar multiplication gates |x⟩ 7 → | ax ⟩ for each a ∈ Fq.However, these gates can be implemented using a constant number of CX ∗ gates along with a constant number of ancilla qudits initialized to |0⟩.We can always collect all X (resp. Z) classical function gates in each timestep into a single larger such classical function gate. This procedure, which we call CF -normalization in Definition 3.21 below, preserves essentially all of the circuit’s properties, but slightly increases the set of possible postselections as subsequently described in Remark 3.22. 

Definition 3.21. Let Q = ( Q1, . . . , Q T ; Nin , N out ) be a quantum circuit using gate set G ⊇ CF ∗.We define the CF-normalization of Q to be the circuit NCF (Q) obtained by collecting all CF X, ∗

(resp. CF Z, ∗) gates in each time step Qt into a single CF X, ∗ (resp. CF Z, ∗) gate. That is, for 

α ∈ { X, Z }, if CF α,f 1 , . . . , CF α,f ℓ are the CF α, ∗ gates in a given time step Qt, we define NCF (Q) to instead invoke the combined gate CF α, L  

> i∈[ℓ]fi

. The remaning (non-CF ∗) gates, as well as the space and time usage, of NCF (Q) are idential to those of Q. We say Q is CF-normalized if NCF (Q) = Q.Remark 3.22 . By Definition 3.13 we have

CF α,f i = CF α, L

Hence CF-normalization preserves the circuit’s associated superoperator N CF (Q)( ·) = Q(·). How-ever, in general the set of postselected circuit superoperators N CF (Q)[ ∗]( ·) for the CF-normalization may be larger than for the original circuit Q[∗]( ·), as a postselection on the combined gate CF α, L 

> i∈[ℓ]fi

may introduce correlations across different factors, which could not arise as a product of postselec-tions on the factor gates CF α,f i . This slightly larger set of postselections will be useful in the proof of Lemma 6.11, where we compile logical circuits to have a certain desired form. 

3.3.3 Fault-Tolerant Gadgets 

Our goal in this paper is to construct fault-tolerant gadgets , which we define in this section to be circuits that yield the correct output whenever the fault avoids certain bad sets. We begin by formally defining a notion of such bad sets below. 

Definition 3.23. For a set N and a family of subsets E ⊆ 2N called bad sets , we say that a set 

S ⊆ N is E-avoiding if no element of E is contained in S, that is, E̸ ⊆ S for every E ∈ E .We extend this definition to Pauli faults in the natural way, so that a Pauli fault F on a circuit using space ⊆ N and time ≤ T is E-avoiding for E ⊆ 2N ×[T ] if supp( F) is E-avoiding. We then say that a fault F = ( F1, . . . , F T ) is E-avoiding if each Ft can be expressed as a linear combination Ft = Pmt 

> i=1

αt,i Ft,i of Pauli superoperators Ft, 1, . . . , F t,m t for coefficients αt,i ∈ C such that the Pauli fault (F1,i 1 , . . . , F T,i T ) is E-avoiding for every tuple (i1, . . . , i T ) ∈ [m1] × · · · × [mT ].For families E1 ⊆ 2N1 , E2 ⊆ 2N2 , we let E1 ⊔ E 2 ⊆ 2N1⊔N2 denote the disjoint union of E1 and E2

on separate sets of underlying elements. Therefore for E ⊆ 2N and T ∈ N, we write E⊔T ⊆ 2N ×[T ]

to denote E ⊔ · · · ⊔ E (with T copies of E). Remark 3.24 . At first glance, it may seem restrictive that the definition of a E-avoiding fault involves Paulis, as we ultimately want to protect against general noise and not simply Pauli noise. 27 However, recall from Lemma 3.17 that every superoperator can be expressed as a linear combination of Pauli superoperators, with supports inside that of the original superoperator. Therefore we can equivalently say that F = ( F1, . . . , F T ) is a E-avoiding fault if each Ft can be expressed as a linear combination Ft = Pmt 

> i=1

αt,i Ft,i of superoperators Ft, 1, . . . , F t,m t for coefficients αt,i ∈ C such that the support of the fault ( F1,i 1 , . . . , F T,i T ) is E-avoiding for every tuple ( i1, . . . , i T ) ∈ [m1]×· · ·× [mT ]. Therefore Definition 3.23 is actually basis-independent, despite being stated in terms of the Pauli basis for superoperators. To specify a fault-tolerant gadget using a subsystem code C, we will decorate the code with an encoding map Enc, a set Γ of allowed states of the gauge qudits, and a family E of bad sets of errors, as stated in Definition 3.25 below. 

Definition 3.25. Let C = ( CX , C Z ) be a [[ n, k ]] q CSS subsystem code with CSS encoding map 

Enc : Fk+k′ 

> q

→ (CZ + C⊥ 

> X

> q×Fk′

be a set of operators, which we call allowed states of the gauge qudits of C. Let E ⊆ 2[n] be a family of subsets of qudits, which we call bad sets for C. We say the data D = ( C, Enc , Γ, E) forms a decorated CSS subsystem code . If k′ = 0 ,then Γ is trivial, so we say D = ( C, Enc , E) forms a decorated CSS non-subsystem code . We let ∅

denote the trivial decorated code with n = k = 0 .For an operator ρ0 ∈ CFk+k′

, we say an operator σ ∈ CFnq ×Fnq is a Pauli E-deviation of Enc( ρ0) if there exists a Pauli superoperator F : CFnq ×Fnq → CFnq ×Fnq such that σ ∝ F ◦ Enc( ρ0), and such that supp( F ) is E-avoiding. For a set R ⊆ CFk+k′

of superoperators ¯O : CFkq ×Fkq → CFkq ×Fkq , we say that σ is a Pauli E-deviation of Enc( ¯O(ρ) ⊗ Γ) if there exists ¯O ∈ ¯O and γ ∈ Γ such that σ is a Pauli E-deviation of Enc( ¯O(ρ) ⊗ γ).We say σ is a E-deviation of Enc( ρ0) (resp. Enc( R)) if σ can be expressed as a linear combi-nation σ = Pmi=1 σi of Pauli E-deviations σ1, . . . , σ m of Enc( ρ0) (resp. Enc( R)). Remark 3.26 . Similarly as described in Remark 3.24 regarding Definition 3.23, Pauli noise is not intrinsic to Definition 3.25. Specifically, by Lemma 3.17, we can equivalently say that σ is a E-deviation of Enc( ρ0) if σ can be expressed as a linear combination σ = Pmi=1 σi of operators σi

satisfying tr [N ]\[n](σi) ∝ F ◦ Enc( ρ0) for some superoperator F such that supp( F ) is E-avoiding. An analogous Pauli-free formulation also holds for E-deviations of Enc( R). In Definition 3.25, it is helpful to think of Γ to be a set of density operators, though the definition also allows for non-physical operators in Γ. In both Definition 3.23 and Definition 3.25, we will often take E to be the family of all subsets of [ n] of size above some threshold λ, which we denote 2[n]|≥λ as defined below. 

Definition 3.27. For a set N , a family of subsets E ⊆ 2N , and a real number λ, we let 

E| ≥λ = {E ∈ E : |E| ≥ λ}

denote the subfamily of those sets of size ≥ λ.

We are now ready to present our main definition of a fault-tolerant gadget. Such a gadget implements a logical operation ¯O where the input and output are encoded into codes Din and Dout 

respectively. 

Definition 3.29. For α ∈ { in , out }, let Dα = ( Cα, Enc α, Γα, Eα) be a decorated [[ nα, k α]] q subsystem code. Let ¯O be a set of superoperators with kin input qudits and kout output qudits. Let Q =(Q1, . . . , Q T ; Nin , N out ) be a quantum circuit on qudit set N , with associated isomorphisms Nin ∼=[nin ], Nout ∼= [ nout ], and a family of bad sets Erun ⊆ 2N ×[T ]. Here all qudits have a fixed prime power dimension q.We say the data (Q, Erun , D in , D out ) provides a fault-tolerant gadget for ¯O if for every ℓ ∈ N,every operator ρ ∈ CFkin +ℓq ×Fkin +ℓq , every Ein ⊔ [ℓ]-deviation σ ∈ CFnin +ℓq ×Fnin +ℓq of Enc in ⊗Iℓ(ρ ⊗

Γin ), and every Erun -avoiding fault F and postselection ζ for Q we have that Q[F, ζ ] ⊗ Iℓ(σ) ∈

CFnout +ℓq ×Fnout +ℓq is a Eout ⊔ [ℓ]-deviation of (Enc out ◦ ¯O) ⊗ Iℓ(ρ ⊗ Γout ).12 

The gadget is furthermore said to be mending if for every ρ ∈ CFkin +ℓq ×Fkin +ℓq , every [ℓ]-deviation 

σ ∈ CFnin +ℓq ×Fnin +ℓq of Enc in ⊗Iℓ(ρ ⊗ Γin ), and every Erun -avoiding fault F and postselection ζ for 

Q, then Q[F, ζ ] ⊗ Iℓ(σ) ∈ CFnout +ℓq ×Fnout +ℓq is of the form Q[F] ⊗ Iℓ(σ) = Pmi=1 σi, where each σi

is a Eout ⊔ [ℓ]-deviation of (Enc out ◦ ¯O ◦ Li) ⊗ Iℓ(ρ ⊗ Γout ) for some superoperator Li : CFkin   

> q×Fkin
> q

→

CFkin

> q

.

The additional ℓ-qudit system in Definition 3.29, which we call an external system 13 , is required to ensure that fault-tolerance is preserved under parallel composition and captures entanglement with other components in a larger computation, see Section 3.4.2. The mending property 14 is used to remedy leakage from the code space when recursively com-posing fault-tolerant gadgets. That is, a mending gadget will return to the code space under a low-weight fault, even if the input state was far from any code state. 

> 12

As a point of notation, here ρ consists of a kin -qudit register and an ℓ-qudit register (the “external system”). In the expression Enc in ⊗Iℓ(ρ ⊗ Γin ), the channel Enc in acts on the kin -qudit register of ρ along with Γ in , while the channel Iℓ acts on the ℓ-qudit register of ρ. This slight misalignment of register orderings will unfortunately persist in our notation throughout, but the meaning will be clear as Iℓ will always act on the ℓ-qudit external system. Also note that the nin -qudit register of σ ∈ CFnin +ℓq ×Fnin +ℓq corresponds to qudits Nin ∼= [ nin ].

29 3.3.4 Basic Results on Fault-Tolerant Gadgets 

In this section, we present some basic results regarding fault-tolerant gadgets as defined in Defini-tion 3.29. The following lemma shows that for proving (mending) fault-tolerance, it suffices to consider Pauli deviations on the input state, as well as Pauli faults. Similar decompositions of general noise into Pauli noise are used throughout the fault-tolerance literature.

In Lemma 3.32 below, we show that our notion of fault-tolerance in Definition 3.29 implies fault-tolerance against a stronger type of adversary, which is allowed access to its own private side-register. Similarly as in Lemma 3.30, the proof will use a standard Pauli decomposition, which we present for completeness. 

Definition 3.31. Let Q = ( Q1, . . . , Q T ; Nin , N out ) be a quantum circuit acting on a set N of q-dimensional qudits. An extended fault on Q is a sequence F = ( F1, . . . , F T ) of superoperators Ft

acting on qudits N ⊔ A, where A is a set of ancilla q-dimensional qudits. Similarly as with ordinary faults, we say F is an extended Pauli fault if each Ft is a Pauli superoperator. The support supp( F) ⊆ N × [T ] is defined by 

supp( F) = (supp( F1) ∩ N ) ⊔ · · · ⊔ (supp( FT ) ∩ N ).

For E ⊆ 2N ×[T ], we say F is E-avoiding if supp( F) is E-avoiding.

3.4 Gadget Composition 

In this section, we present results showing how fault-tolerance properties are preserved under sequential and parallel composition of gadgets, as defined below. Similar results are shown in [HNP25, NP25]; we simply adapt these results to our setting and notation, and provide proofs for completeness. A more involved form of composition, namely simulative composition, will be addressed in Section 7. 31 3.4.1 Sequential Composition

Lemma 3.34 below considers parallel composition, in which gadgets are run at the same time on disjoint sets of qudits. For simplicity in Lemma 3.34, we assume that all input gadgets have the same running time. In general we could instead pad gadgets with idling timesteps (i.e. identity gates) to ensure this condition is satisfied, though at the cost of potentially increasing the output error.

In this section, we present the subsystem codes underlying our fault-tolerance scheme, and ana-lyze their properties. We construct these codes from tensor products of Reed-Solomon codes, or equivalently, multivariate polynomial evaluation codes, over a large alphabet.

# 5 Core Gadgets over Large Alphabets 

In this section, we present our core fault-tolerant gadgets over large alphabets, implementing error correction (Section 5.1), initialization (Section 5.2), code switching (Section 5.3), logical gates Hadamard (Section 5.4), Paulis (Section 5.5), CNOTs (Section 5.6), Toffolis (Section 5.7), as well as classical function gates (Section 5.8). Throughout this section we assume that all qudits have the same prime power dimension q, which may be growing in other parameters such as the block lengths of the relevant codes. We will later show how to recursively compose these gadgets to reduce the qudit dimension. 

Remark 5.1 . The fault-tolerant gadgets in this section are provided in Lemmas 5.2, 5.4, 5.7, 5.11, 5.13, 5.14, 5.16 and 5.18. In each of these lemmas, we fix a parameter λrun specifying the maximum weight per timestep of a fault under which the gadget returns an appropriate deviation of the desired output state. However, the physical circuit in each of these gadgets does not depend on the choice of λrun .

5.1 Error Correction 

In this section, we present our error-correction gadget for subsystem product codes. We con-struct a quantum circuit Q that performs error correction in the X and Z basis sequentially. The construction crucially makes use of the local testability of tensor products of classical codes (see Proposition 3.3) to deal with measurement errors.

> q

> α′

On input σ ∈ CFNq ×FNq , letting Qα denote the circuit ErrCorr( σ; α, (Hiα)i∈[u], Dec α) (see Algo-rithm 2), then we define our error-correction circuit Q to invoke QX and followed by QZ . In words, for each basis α ∈ { X, Z }, we have defined Q to do the following: 1. Perform syndrome extraction on the parity-check matrix Hα

2. Compute the nearest valid syndrome s′ to the measured syndrome s

3. Compute the vector c inducing syndrome s′ = Hα(c) that is as close as possible to C⊥

> α′

4. Apply a Pauli correction α′− c to the code state As we can think of elements of C⊥ 

> α′

as gauge operators, the correction c can be viewed as the sum of a gauge operator with the lowest possible weight error. We now analyze the space and time usage of Q. Recall here that every ni = n. Proof of Claim 5.3. The first timestep of QZ by definition initializes ancilla qudits to |0⟩ ⟨ 0|, while leaving the input qudits labeled N ⊔ [ℓ] in the state σ. The remainder of QZ simply consists of CX gates, along with a single classical function gate. By definition, conjugating a X or Z Pauli operator by a CX gate yields another X or Z Pauli operator, respectively. Furthermore, such conjugation can only expand the support of a Pauli X (resp. Pauli Z) if the original operator’s support contained the control (resp. target) qudit. It follows that just prior to executing the classical function gate in Algorithm 2 of Algorithm 2, the algorithm’s state is proportional to

This establishes Claim 5.3 and thus we have finished the proof of Lemma 5.2.

Item 1 has trivial input code Din , i.e. no input qudits. In contrast, Item 2 applies Init ∗ gates to an input code state and performing a “reset” operation, thereby tracing out the logical input qudits. Because we allow errors that are non-physical (i.e. non-channel) superoperators, Item 2 requires a more careful analysis to ensure that logical errors are not introduced to any “external system” that is entangled with the input code state.

Thus we have shown that in the absence of a fault, when Q is given as input the state ρ

encoded in Din , it uses the desired space N ′ and time T , and outputs ρ encoded in Dout .It only remains to analyze the behavior of Q under input errors and faults; there are no CF ∗

gates and therefore no postselections to consider. By Lemma 3.30, it suffices to show for every Pauli Ein ⊔ [ℓ]-deviation σ of Enc in ⊗Iℓ(ρ ⊗ Γin ) and every Erun -avoiding Pauli fault F for Q,then Q[F] ⊗ Iℓ(σ) is a Eout ⊔ [ℓ]-deviation of Enc out ⊗Iℓ(ρ ⊗ Γout ). After the Init Z gates in the first timestep, all gates in Q are CX, which are Clifford, meaning that conjugating a Pauli by a CX gate yields another Pauli. Thus the output Q[F] ⊗ Iℓ(σ) must be a Pauli deviation of the output from the noiseless execution, which we showed above is Enc out ⊗Iℓ(ρ ⊗ Γout ). Furthermore, as all CX gates are performed within a direction-¯ i column, such conjugation can only propagate Pauli errors within direction-¯ i columns. As there are < λ in qudits in the support of the input error and < T λ run qudits in the support of the fault, and every direction-¯ i column has n¯i qudits, the weight of the output error is less than 

n¯i(λin + T λ run ) ≤ λout ,

as desired. 2. (Upwards switching) The desired circuit Q will simply encode the ¯ ith factor of the product code C[u]. In this section, we present a basic gadget for a logical Hadamard gate by applying physical Hadamard gates to all qudits in a code state. Note that this gadget outputs a state encoded in a dualized version C⊥ = ( CZ , C X ) of the input code C = ( CX , C Z ). We will later show how to return to the original code by repeated applications of the code switching and error correction gadgets in Lemma 5.7 and Lemma 5.2, respectively. We will first need the notion of dual encoding maps for dualized codes in Definition 5.9 below. Below, for subpaces A, B ⊆ FNq with A⊥ ⊆ B, and for cosets a + B⊥ ∈ A/B ⊥, b + A⊥ ∈ B/A ⊥,we write ( a + B⊥) · (b + A⊥) = a · b ∈ Fq. This notation is well-defined because the dot product of every a + b′ ∈ a + B⊥ with every b + a′ ∈ b + A⊥ equals ( a + b′) · (b + a′) = a · b.

Hence the output error has weight < 2λin + λrun = λout .

5.7 CCX 

In this section, we present a gadget that applies CCX a to all qudits across three code states, assuming that the three (possibly different) codes satisfy an appropriate multiplicative structure. Below, recall from Section 3.1 that for x, y ∈ Fnq , we let x ∗ y = ( xiyi)i∈[n] ∈ Fnq denote the component-wise product. We also extend this notation to sets X, Y ⊆ Fnq , so that X ∗ Y = {x ∗ y :

x ∈ X, y ∈ Y }.

where the second equality above holds by Equation (36) along with the linearity of Enc 3.Thus we have shown that Q has the desired behavior of applying ¯O in the absence of errors. It then follows by Lemma 3.30 that ( Q, Erun , D in , D out ) is a fault-tolerant gadget for ¯O, as Q applies 3-qudit unitary gates for a single timestep. Specifically, an output qudit labeled i ∈ [n] in any of the three n-qudit blocks can receive an error only if that qudit was one of the < λ run qudits in the support of the (Pauli) fault, or if any of the qudits labeled i across the three n-qudit blocks was one of the < λ in qudits in the support of the input (Pauli) error. Hence the output error has weight 

< 3λin + λrun = λout . Note that as CCX is non-Clifford, even under Pauli input and fault errors, the output error may be given by non-Pauli unitaries, though by Lemma 3.17 it can be decomposed into a sum of Pauli errors with the same (or smaller) supports. The following lemma shows that a triple of subsystem product codes satisfies the condition in Equation (36) if the respective triples of factor codes satisfy this condition.

5.8 Classical Function Gates 

In this section, we present a gadget for performing a logical classical function gate on an entire code state. We will later show how to perform logical permutations that allow us to apply such gates on desired subsets of the logical qudits.

u4n2 log 2 n), and gate set G.19 Furthermore, Q does not depend on the choice of λrun .Remark 6.2 . In Item 2 in Theorem 6.1, it would be sufficient to consider Din , D out each given by disjoint unions of 2 (instead of 3) codes, as we can sequentially compose gadgets with |Kin ,1| +

|Kin ,2| = |Kout ,1| to accumulate the logical qudits across any number m of code states into a single code state. Similarly, we can compose gadgets with |Kin ,1| = |Kout ,1| + |Kout ,2| to distribute the logical qudits from one code state across m code states. We choose to consider disjoint unions of 3 codes in Theorem 6.1 for notational convenience with the CCX gate, which acts on 3 qudits. 

Remark 6.3 . Item 2 in Theorem 6.1 can be generalized to allow Din ,b and Dout ,b to be given by codes C(q, u, n, k, K in ,b ) and C(q, u ′, n ′, k ′, K out ,b ) respectively with different values of ( u, n, k )̸ =(u′, n ′, k ′). However, we restrict to the case where ( u, n, k ) = ( u′, n ′, k ′) for simplicity.

The remainder of this section is dedicated to proving Theorem 6.1. As in Section 5, in this section we assume that all qudits have the same prime power dimension q, which may be growing in other parameters such as the block lengths of the relevant codes.

6.2 Logical Circuit Compilation 

In this section, we show how to compile an arbitrary logical circuit ¯Q into one whose structure is amenable to our fault-tolerant gadgets in Section 5, with only small blowups in the space and time usage. This compilation is similar to the one used in the classical fault-tolerance scheme of [ABG +26]. We first need the following notation. 

Definition 6.10. For a set K = Q

> i∈[u]

[ki], for every i ∈ [u] and j ∈ [ki] we define a subset 

K|i→j ⊆ K by K|i→j = {κ ∈ K : κi = j}.Given an m-qudit gate G and disjoint sets of qudits S1 ∼= · · · ∼= Sm with fixed isomorphisms, the 

transversal application GS1,...,S m of G to S1, . . . , S m is the m|Sb|-qudit gate given by applying G to every m-tuple of qudits s1 ∈ S1, . . . , s m ∈ Sm that are associated under the fixed isomorphisms. In particular, the transversal application of G to K|i→j1 , . . . , K |i→jm applies G to all m-tuples of qudits in K|i→j1 × · · · × K|i→jm whose labels agree on all coordinates in [u] \ { i}.
