To prove Lemma 6.11, we will use the following known result on efficiently implementing per-mutations in circuits with a hypercubic connectivity structure. Below, we define the unitary gate Swap ∈ CF2  

> q×F2
> q

in the ordinary way Swap |x1, x 2⟩ = |x2, x 1⟩. Recall that Swap can for instance be implemented with 6 CX ∗ gates, using an ancilla qudit initialized to |0⟩.23 

Lemma 6.12 ([Bat68]) . For s ∈ N, let S = [2] s, and let π : S → S be a permutation. Then there exists a circuit Q acting on qudits labeled by S using time T ≤ O(s2) and gate set {Swap },such that Q(·) implements π, that is, Q(|x⟩ ⟨ x′|) = |π(x)⟩ ⟨ π(x′)|. Furthermore, for every time step 

t ∈ [T ], there exists some i(t) ∈ [s] such that every Swap gate in Qt acts on a pair of qudits whose labels differ only in coordinate i(t).

Lemma 6.12 allows us to implement arbitrary logical permutations via transversal controlled-swap gates, which in turn can be implemented using transversal CCX gates. Therefore to prove Lemma 6.11, we will use Lemma 6.12 to move qudits into appropriate positions, such that the desired gates can then be implemented transversally. We provide the details below; we will also use the following basic fact. 

Fact 6.13. For every a ∈ Fq, the gate CX a can be implemented using one ancilla qudit initialized to |0⟩ along with one CCX +1 gate and two X∗ gates. Furthermore, the gate CCX a can be implemented using two ancilla qudits initialized to |0⟩ along with two CCX +1 gates, one CCX −1 gate, and two X∗ gates. Proof. We implement CX a by performing 

|x1, x 2, 0⟩ Xa

−−→ | x1, x 2, a ⟩ CCX +1 

−−−−→ | x1, x 2 + ax 1, a ⟩ X−a

−−−→ | x1, x 2, 0⟩ .

Proof of Lemma 6.11. For B ⊆ [¯b], we let KB = F 

> b∈B

Kb. Also let data = {1, . . . , 4u} and anc = 

{4u + 1 , . . . , 2 · 4u}. We assume the blocks K1, . . . , K 4u are arranged in a u-dimensional hypercube of side length 4, so that we have a fixed isomorphism [4 k]u ∼= ([4] × [k]) u ∼= Kdata . Under this isomorphism, we assume that K[16] ⊆ [2 k]u (which is possible as u ≥ 4). Let s = ⌈log 2 k⌉ + 1, and fix an isomorphism [2 s] ∼= [2] s, so that [2 s]u ∼= [2] su . Note that 2 k ≤ 2s ≤ 4k.We begin by applying Lemma 6.12 to show Claim 6.14 below. In the remainder of this proof below, we say a circuit acting on dits K[¯b] = Kdata ⊔ Kanc (or on a subset thereof) has transver-sal structure if each timestep of the circuit has the structure required of ¯Q′ 

> t

in the statement of Lemma 6.11. 

Claim 6.14. For every permutation π : [2 s]u×[2 s]u, there exists a circuit using gate set {Init Z , X∗, CCX ∗} ⊆ G with transversal structure that permutes the qudits in [2 s]u by π in time O(su )2. Qudits in 

K[¯b] \ [2 s]u may be acted upon arbitrarily. Proof. Lemma 6.12 gives a circuit on qudits [2 s]u ∼= [2] su that implements the permutation π

using time O(su )2 and gate set {Swap }, where each timestep t applies a subset of the Swap gates performed in transversal applications to sets Kb|i→j for some i = i(t) ∈ [u]. That is, for various disjoint pairs of sets Kb1 |i→j1 , K b2 |i→j2 ⊆ Kdata , this circuit from Lemma 6.12 applies Swap to some (but maybe not all) pairs of qudits κ1 ∈ Kb1 |i→j1 , κ 2 ∈ Kb2 |i→j2 whose labels in [ k]u agree on coordinates [ u] \ { i}. For each such ( b1, j 1), (b2, j 2), we may instead initialize a block Kb3 |i→j3 ⊆

Kanc to contain the indicator vector for the Swap gates we want to perform on Kb1 |i→j1 , K b2 |i→j2 .This initialization can be done with one layer of Init Z gates followed by one layer of X ∗ gates. Then the desired Swap gates can be implemented by performing transversal controlled-Swap across the three blocks Kb3 |i→j3 , K b1 |i→j1 , K b2 |i→j2 . Just as Swap can be implemented using an ancilla qudit initialized to |0⟩ along with 6 CX ∗ gates, controlled-Swap can be implemented using an ancilla qudit initialized to |0⟩ along with 6 CCX ∗ gates. Therefore we initialize an additional block Kb4 |i→j4 ⊆ Kanc for these additional ancilla qudits, and then we implement one layer of the desired Swap gates using gates {Init Z , X∗, CCX ∗} ⊆ G in a circuit with transversal structure. Furthermore, by construction this circuit acts on dits K[¯b] (as the number of ancilla blocks in Kanc 

we use equals the number of data blocks in Kdata ) and runs in time O(1). It follows that the entire permutation circuit, which consists of O(su )2 layers of Swap gates, can be implemented in a circuit with transversal structure using gate set {Init Z , X∗, CCX ∗} ⊆ G in time O(su )2, as desired. We thus compile N CF ( ¯Q) into ¯Q′ as follows. First, increasing the running time by at most a factor of 5, we can assume that every timestep in ¯Q (and hence in N CF ( ¯Q)) consists entirely of gates from one of the sets X ∗, Z ∗, H ∗, CX ∗, CCX ∗, Init X , Init Z , CF X, ∗, or CF Z, ∗. We now compile each such timestep into a sub-circuit of ¯Q′ of the desired transversal structure, using time 

O(su )2. Specifically, we consider timesteps of N CF ( ¯Q) with each type of gates separately, where below α ∈ { X, Z }:71 1. X ∗ (resp. Z ∗): Simply apply the desired X ∗ (resp. Z ∗) gates to qudits in K[3] .2. H ∗ (resp. Init α) on qudits A ⊆ K1,2,3: Use Claim 6.14 to swap some |A| qudits in K4,5,6 ⊆

Kdata with the qudits A ⊆ K1,2,3, while preserving the positions of the other qudits in K[6] .Then run H ∗ (resp. Init α) on K4,5,6, and subsequently apply Claim 6.14 again to permute the qudits in K[6] back to their original positions. 3. CX ∗ on various pairs of qudits in K1,2,3: First, apply Claim 6.14 to permute the qudits in K[6] 

such that every CX ∗ gate now has control qudit in K1,2,3, and target qudit in the respective position in K4,5,6. For qudits acted on the by identity, we simply assign their associated gate to be CX 0 = I2. We then implement the desired CX ∗ gates using transversal gates in G

on K1, . . . , K 9. Specifically, we implement each gate CX a using the procedure in Fact 6.13, where K7,8,9 provides the ancilla qudits, so that each timestep either performs X ∗ gates, or else performs transversal CCX +1 or Init Z gates across different blocks Kb. Finally, we apply Claim 6.14 again to permute the qudits in K[6] back to their original positions. 4. CCX ∗ on various triples of qudits in K1,2,3: First, apply Claim 6.14 to permute the qudits in 

K[9] such that every CX ∗ gate now has first control qudit in K1,2,3, second control qudit in the respective position in K4,5,6, and target qudit in the respective position in K7,8,9. For qudits acted on the by identity, we simply assign their associated gate to be CCX 0 = I3. We then implement the desired CCX ∗ gates using transversal gates in G on K1, . . . , K 15 . Specifically, we implement each gate CCX a using the procedure in Fact 6.13, where K10 ,..., 16 provides the ancilla qudits, so that each timestep either performs X ∗ gates, or else performs transversal CCX +1 , CCX −1, or Init gates across different blocks Kb. Finally, we apply Claim 6.14 again to permute the qudits in K[9] back to their original positions. 5. CF α, ∗ on qudits A ⊆ K1,2,3: First, run Init α to every qudit in K4,5,6. Then we use Claim 6.14 to swap some |A| qudits in K4,5,6 with the qudits A ⊆ K1,2,3, while preserving the positions of the other qudits in K[6] . By the definition of CF-normalization in Definition 3.21, N CF ( ¯Q)can only apply a single CF α, ∗ gate in a timestep. Thus we can perform in ¯Q′ a single such gate in CF α, ∗ on K4,5,6, in order to induce the same action as the associated CF α, ∗ gate in ¯Q. Finally, we apply Claim 6.14 again to permute the qudits in K[6] back to their original positions. Due to our initialization of qudits in K4,5,6 to α-basis states as described above, the effect of an arbitrary postselection ζ on our CF α, ∗ gate in ¯Q′ is equivalent to that of the restricted postselection ζ|FAq on the respective CF α, ∗ gate in N CF ( ¯Q), where this restriction implicitly applies to the permuted locations of the qudits in A.By construction, in all cases above, the resulting circuit acting on qudits K[¯b] has the desired transversal structure, and by Claim 6.14 uses time O(su )2. For each timestep of the original circuit NCF ( ¯Q), the above construction for ¯Q′ by definition induces the same superoperator, even when including postselections. Therefore ¯Q′(·) = ¯Q(·) = N CF ( ¯Q)( ·) and ¯Q′[∗]( ·) = N CF ( ¯Q)[ ∗]( ·), as desired. Note that Item 5 in the proof of Lemma 6.11 above required using the CF-normalization N CF ( ¯Q)in place of the original circuit ¯Q due to the reasoning described in Remark 3.22. That is, postse-lections on the single combined CF ∗ gate in a given timestep of ¯Q′ could introduce correlations not attainable as the product of postselections on the associated CF ∗ gates in ¯Q.72 6.3 Fault-Tolerant Circuits 

In this section, we apply our gadgets in Section 5 to fault-tolerantly implement circuits given by the compiler in Lemma 6.11, thereby completing the proof of Theorem 6.1. Throughout this section, we define q, u, n, k, λ run , ¯λrun , λ min , λ max , d = ( d1, . . . , d u) as in Theorem 6.1, we define 

C∗(q, u, n, k ), C (q, u, n, k, K ) as in Definition 6.9, we define D(q, u, n, k, λ run ) as in Definition 6.4, and we define ¯b = 2 · 4u as in Lemma 6.11. We begin by defining a family D′(q, u, n, k, λ run ) of subsystem codes in Definition 6.15 below, which we will use to perform the fault-tolerant computations. These codes are closely related to the codes C(q, u, n, k, K ) in Definition 6.9; indeed, Lemma 6.19 below shows how to fault-tolerantly switch between the two families of codes.

Proof of Lemma 6.16. 75 Proof. The desired circuit Q will simply be a serial composition of gadgets in Lemma 6.16 using the choice in Item 2b or Item 2c. It will therefore follow by Remark 6.17 that Q does not depend on the choice of λrun or on the families of bad sets. Specifically, for α ∈ { in , out }, let Dα = ( Cα, Enc α, Γα, Eα), so that ( Cα, Enc α) ∈ B Iα (q, u, n, k )for some Iα ⊆ [u] of size |Iα| ≥ u − 2, with ( Cα, Enc α) ∈ B Iα;iα (q, u, n, k ) for some iα ∈ [u] \ I

if |Iα| = u − 2. If |Iin | = u then Q first applies the gadget in Lemma 6.16 using Item 2b, while if |Iin | = u − 2 then Q first applies the gadget in Lemma 6.16 using Item 2c; in either case, Q

has switched from Din to some code D0 = ( C0, Enc 0, Γ0, E0) ∈ D ′(q, u, n, k, λ run ) with ( C0, Enc 0) ∈BI (q, u, n, k ) for some I ⊆ [u] of size |I| = u − 1. Now we can switch from D0 to any other code D1 = ( C1, Enc 1, Γ1, E1) ∈ D ′(q, u, n, k, λ run ) with (C1, Enc 1) ∈ B I (q, u, n, k ). To do so, we loop through i ∈ I, and for every i we apply Lemma 6.16 twice, using Item 2b followed by Item 2c, with both switches applied to the ith factor of the product codes. Specifically, for α ∈ N ∪ { in , out } writing (Cα, Enc α) = G

# 7 Alphabet Reduction via Simulative Composition 

In this section, we show how to perform simulative composition on our fault-tolerance scheme in Theorem 6.1. That is, we show how to fault-tolerantly simulate high-dimensional qudits using The-orem 6.1 over lower-dimensional qudits. This composition allows us to reduce the qudit dimension of our fault-tolerance scheme, which is exponentially large in Theorem 6.1, down to an arbitrary constant prime power. 

7.1 Main Result Statement 

We state our main result in Theorem 7.1 below. We will build up the necessary simulative compo-sition machinery in the subsequent subsections, before proving Theorem 7.1 in Section 7.4. 

Theorem 7.1. For every prime power r, every 0 < ϵ < 1/8, and every ηgap ≥ 0, there exists 

¯N0 = ¯N0(r, ϵ ) ≥ 0 such that the following holds. For every set ¯N of size | ¯N | ≥ ¯N0 and every subset K ⊆ ¯N , there is a CSS non-subsystem code C(r, ϵ, K, ¯N ) with associated encoding map 

Enc C(r,ϵ,K, ¯N ), which satisfy the following for some integer M = M (r, ϵ, ¯N ) ≤ | ¯N |4+2 9ϵ:1. C(r, ϵ, K, ¯N ) is a [[ n(r, ϵ, ¯N ), k = |K|, d(r, ϵ, ¯N )]] r code with logical qudits labeled by the set K, where 

n(r, ϵ, ¯N ) ≤ 232 √log | ¯N | · M · | ¯N |

d(r, ϵ, ¯N ) ≥ 2−29√log | ¯N | · M · | ¯N |.

2. For every CF -normalized 24 quantum circuit ¯Q = ( ¯Q1, . . . , ¯Q ¯T ; Kin , K out ) acting on a set of r-dimensional qudits labeled by ¯N using time ¯T and gate set G = {H∗, X∗, Z∗, CX ∗, CCX ∗, Init ∗, CF ∗},letting 

Din = ( Cin = C(r, ϵ, K in , ¯N ), Enc in = Enc Cin , Ein = 2 [n(r,ϵ, ¯N )] |≥λin )

Dout = ( Cout = C(r, ϵ, K out , ¯N ), Enc out = Enc Cout , Eout = 2 [n(r,ϵ, ¯N )] |≥λout ),

> 24 As described in Section 3.3.2, this CF-normalization requirement comes without any loss in generality (except in the set of permitted postselections), as arbitrary logical circuits can be CF-normalized with no loss in parameters.

82 then there exists a fault-tolerant gadget 

 Q, Erun = 2 N |≥λrun , D in , D out 



for ¯Q[∗]( ·), where Q is a quantum circuit using space |N | ≤ 2O(√log | ¯N |) · M · | ¯N |, time 

T ≤ 229√log | ¯N | · ¯T , and gate set G, and where 

λin = λin (r, ϵ, η gap , ¯N ) ≥ 2−211 √log | ¯N | · M · | ¯N |

λrun = λrun (r, ϵ, η gap , ¯N ) ≥ 2−2ηgap+ O(1) √log | ¯N | · M · | ¯N |

λout = λout (r, ϵ, η gap , ¯N ) ≤ 2−2ηgap+62 √log | ¯N | · M · | ¯N |.

We have not attempted to optimize constants in Theorem 7.1, and give some explicit values just for concreteness and clarity in the proof. We emphasize that while Theorem 7.1 only fault-tolerantly implements the logical circuit ¯Q

up to arbitrary postselections on the logical classical function gates, we can implement logical circuits consisting of unitary gates and mid-circuit measurements without any classical function gates, and hence without any postselection. In particular, as described in Section 2.2.4, we can perform mid-circuit logical measurements using Init ∗ and CX ∗ gates, rather than CF ∗ gates. 

7.2 Increasing Logical Alphabet Size 

In this section, we show how to perform a quantum computation over a small field Fq using qudits over an arbitrary extension field Fq′ .

Lemma 7.2. Let Fq be a finite field, let Fq′ be an extension field, and let ι : Fq → Fq′ denote the natural inclusion. We also let ι : CFq ×Fq → CFq′ ×Fq′ denote the associated quantum channel given by ι(|x⟩ ⟨ y|) = |ι(x)⟩ ⟨ ι(y)|.Let ¯Q = ( ¯Q1, . . . , ¯Q ¯T ; Kin , K out ) be a quantum circuit acting on q-dimensional qudits labeled by a set ¯N , using time ¯T and gate set G = {H∗, X∗, Z∗, CX ∗, CCX ∗, Init ∗, CF ∗} (over Fq). Then there exists a quantum circuit ¯Q′ = ( ¯Q′

> 1

, . . . , ¯Q′¯T ′ ; Kin , K out ) acting on q′-dimensional qudits labeled by a set ¯N ′ ⊇ ¯N with | ¯N ′| ≤ | ¯N | · 8 log 2 q, using time ¯T ′ ≤ ¯T · O(log q) and gate set G (over Fq′ ), such that for every postselection ζ′ for ¯Q′, there exists a postselection ζ for ¯Q such that 

¯Q′[ζ′] ◦ ι⊔Kin ∝ ι⊔Kout ◦ ¯Q[ζ]. (47) 

In the equation above, ¯Q[ζ], ¯Q′[ζ′] denote the superoperators associated to these respective postse-lected circuits. Furthermore, if ¯Q is CF -normalized, then ¯Q′ is also CF -normalized. Proof. First, we may replace each timestep in ¯Q with three timesteps, to ensure that each timestep either contains no CF ∗ gates, or else consists entirely of CF X, ∗ or of CF Z, ∗ gates. We then again triple the number of timesteps to replace all Z ∗, Init X, ∗, CF X, ∗ gates with X ∗, Init Z, ∗, CF Z, ∗ gates, respectively, conjugated by Hadamard gates. By Definition 3.13 and Fact 3.14, such a change does not change the space usage of ¯Q, increases the time usage by at most a factor of 9, and does not change the associated channel ¯Q(·). Therefore we may assume that ¯Q in fact only uses gate set 

G′ = {H∗, X∗, CX ∗, CCX ∗, Init Z, ∗, CF Z, ∗}, and does not perform CF X, ∗ or CF Z, ∗ gates in the same timestep as any other type of gate. 83 Now for every gate G ∈ G ′ over Fq acting on some number b of qudits, we will construct a circuit 

G′ over Fq′ with b input and output qudits, such that for every postselection ˜ζ′ for G′, there exists a postselection ˜ζ for G with 

G′[˜ζ′] ◦ ι⊔b ∝ ι⊔b ◦ G[˜ζ]. (48) Note that if G or G′ does not include CF ∗ gates, the associated postselection is trivial. Our final circuit ¯Q′ will then be obtained by replacing each gate G in ¯Q with the corresponding circuit G′

in ¯Q′. ¯Q′ will act on a set of data qudits labeled by ¯N that correspond to the respective qudits of ¯Q, along with a set of ancilla qudits used by the circuits G′ (specifically for G ∈ H∗). If ¯Q is CF-normalized, we also collect all CF gates in each timestep of ¯Q′ into a single CF gate (see Definition 3.21) to ensure that ¯Q′ is also CF-normalized. Such CF-normalization will only be necessary for timesteps in which ¯Q does not perform any CF ∗ gates (as timesteps with a CF ∗

gate in ¯Q will naturally have just a single associated CF ∗ gate in ¯Q′), so ˜ζ is trivial within these timesteps. Hence this CF-normalization will not affect the correctness of Equation (48). By definition for every gate G ∈ G ′ \ H∗ over Fq, Equation (48) holds when G′ is the circuit that applies a single gate given by the Fq′ -version of G. Specifically, when G = CF ∗ acts on some number m of qudits, then we take ˜ζ = ˜ζ′|Fmq (49) using the natural inclusion Fmq ⊆ Fmq′ , and when G ∈ G ′ \ { H∗, CF ∗}, then both ˜ζ, ˜ζ′ are trivial. Therefore it only remains to consider G ∈ H∗. Below we construct G′ for G = H; the G = H † case is analogous. When G = H, we define G′ = H ′ to perform the following steps, given as input a single q′-dimensional qudit. Below, we let p denote the characteristic of Fq, Fq′ , we let ωp = e2πi/p denote the pth root of unity, and we let β ∈ Fq′ be an arbitrary element satisfying tr Fq′ /Fq (β) = 1. 7.3 Reducing Physical Alphabet Size 

In this section, we show how to reduce the physical alphabet size of a fault-tolerance scheme. The main idea is to perform simulative composition to fault-tolerantly simulate each large-alphabet gate using Theorem 6.1. We are able to work with non-subsystem codes for simplicity, as Theorem 6.1 provides such codes. In particular, Lemma 7.15 below is the critical ingredient for proving Theorem 7.1. It shows that from a fault-tolerant gadget over a large field Fq′ we can construct a fault-tolerant gadget over a smaller subfield Fq. To prove Theorem 7.1, we will recursively apply Lemma 7.15 (or more precisely, the instantiation in Corollary 7.18) until the large alphabet size required by the top-level code of the gadget in Theorem 6.1 is reduced to a constant size. Throughout this section, we use the following basic notation, which fixes an isomorphism be-tween an extension field and the corresponding vector space over the base field. 

Definition 7.3. For every prime power q and every κ ∈ N, letting q′ = qκ, then we let ϕq,q ′ :

Fκq

> ∼

−→ Fq′ denote an arbitrary fixed Fq-linear isomorphism. We also let ϕq,q ′ : CFκq ∼

ϕq,q ′ : CFκq ×Fκq ∼

−→ CFq′ ×Fq′ denote the ismorphisms ϕ |x⟩ = |ϕ(x)⟩ and ϕ(|x⟩ ⟨ x′|) = |ϕ(x)⟩ ⟨ ϕ(y)|

respectively; the use will be clear from context. 

In Lemma 7.4 below, for a prime power q and a larger prime power q′ = qκ with κ ∈ N, we show how to non-fault-tolerantly simulate the gates {H∗, X∗, Z∗, CX ∗, CCX ∗, Init ∗} over q′-dimensional qudits using the analogous gates over q-dimensional qudits. We will subsequently apply Theorem 6.1 to these simulation circuits to obtain fault-tolerance. Note that this application will have κ growing polynomially in the number of qudits in our overall fault-tolerant circuit. To ensure that our fault-tolerance scheme retains subpolynomial time overhead and almost-linear error resilience, we therefore need the simulation in Lemma 7.4 below to have time usage growing subpolynomially in 

κ. To obtain such a low (specifically, logarithmic) time overhead in our simulation, we incur a larger (polynomial) space overhead than would otherwise be needed. Specifically, the key element of our simulation of CX ∗, CCX ∗, H∗ gates is given in Claim 7.5 below, where we show how to implement linear and bilinear maps on Fκq using space poly( κ) and time O(log κ).

If the target qudit of the CX a gate is not a leaf of the tree, we choose the coefficient a = 1. If the target is a leaf (say the jth leaf for j ∈ [n]), we choose a = Aj,i . This procedure by definition takes ≤ 2(log( n) + 1) time, as each round takes two timesteps (one for the first child of each node in that round, and one for the second child). Next, for each j ∈ [n], we define a depth ≤ log( m) + 1 binary tree on qudits with m leaves given by the qudits |Aj,i xi⟩ for i ∈ [m]. The internal nodes of this tree are all fresh ancilla qudits initialized to |0⟩. We then perform ≤ log( m) + 1 rounds of CX 1 gates, where in the tth round we apply CX 1 from every qudit at distance t − 1 from a leaf of this tree to its parent. This procedure by definition takes ≤ 2(log( m) + 1) time, and leaves the root of the jth tree in the state

Remark 7.7 . By Theorem 6.1, the circuit QG,D in ,D out ,T in Definition 7.6 does not depend on the choice of λrun .We now define a condition called smooth-mending for gadgets given by the circuits QG,D in ,D out ,T 

defined in Definition 7.6, where G ∈ CF Z, ∗ is a classical function gate. We cannot simply use the ordinary mending property for classical function gates because such gates may act on arbitrarily many qudits. Therefore in our fault-tolerant circuit QG,D in ,D out ,T simulating a large-alphabet gate 

G, if a small fraction of the input blocks have large uncorrectable corruptions, we still want to simulate the desired gate G with errors on a small fraction of the inputs. In contrast, the ordinary 

> 27 See Footnote 17.

90 mending property is bimodal with respect to uncorrectable corruptions: either there is no uncor-rectable corruption, in which case the desired logical operation is performed, or else there is an uncorrectable corruption, in which case a logical error may occur on all input qudits. Specifically, letting G act on ¯b qudits, then we let Bin ⊆ [¯b] denote the set of input blocks of 

QG,D in ,D out ,T with “good” (i.e. correctable) corruptions, and we let Brun ⊆ [¯b] denote the set of blocks that experience good faults. Roughly speaking, we then define our gadget to be smoothly-mending if it fault-tolerantly simulates the desired gate G up to logical errors on inputs in Bin , and bad corruptions on outputs in Brun .Our precise definition of a bad fault for a smoothly-mending gadget does not allow for any errors during the execution of the initialization and error-correction subroutines (see the description of 

QG,D in ,D out ,T above), but allows arbitrary errors outside of these subroutines. We will justify this definition by replacing the true fault with an extended fault (see Definition 3.31) that induces the same corruption, but has no support inside these subroutines. The main idea is to allow the adversary to swap out the relevant qudits to a private register, apply their corrupted subroutine there, and then swap the qudits back; the original subroutine is then only corrupted by the swap gates at the start and end.

The following lemma is well known, though we provide a brief proof sketch for intuition. 

Lemma 7.14 (Well known) . The concatenated code ˜C defined in Definition 7.13 has distance dd ′.Proof sketch. Assume for a contradiction that the physical realization of some nontrivial logical Pauli operator L acting on ˜C has weight < dd ′. By definition, a code state of ˜C consists of n′

(entangled) code states of C, each of which in turn contains n qudits. Therefore the restriction of L

to each of these n′ code states of C must be a logical operator for C, and hence either have weight 

≥ d or else act trivially on the code space. Now if < d ′ of these n′ restrictions of L have weight ≥ d,then ( ϕq,q ′ ◦ Enc −1)⊔n′

◦ L ◦ (Enc ◦ϕ−1 

> q,q ′

)⊔n′

acts nontrivially on < d ′ components of C′, and hence must act trivially on the code space of C′, a contradiction. Therefore we must instead have ≥ d′ of the n′ restrictions of L to code states of C have weight ≥ d, meaning that L has weight ≥ dd ′.

As discussed above, our proof of Lemma 7.15 below uses swap gates to transform a general Pauli fault on the composed circuit ˜Q into an extended fault with a nicer structure (see Definition 3.31). A similar technique was used to deal with general adversarial noise in [HNP25].

Specifically, each of these pairs of swaps is sandwiched by Init Z gates on the involved qudits in ˜A, and the postselection ˜ζ′ on the associated region in ˜N is trivial. Hence up to relabeling of some qudits from the swap gates, ˜Q[ ˜F′, ˜ζ′]( ·) simply runs ˜Q[ ˜F, ˜ζ]( ·), while sometimes initializing an additional block of qudits in the |0⟩ state, applying QG,D in ,D out ,T , Q1, or Q2 (all of which are channels due to the trivial postselection), and then tracing out the channel’s output. These additional initializations, channels, and tracing-outs have no effect on the execution of ˜Q[ ˜F, ˜ζ]( ·), as desired. Therefore it suffices to show that the resulting output ˜Q[ ˜F′, ˜ζ′] ⊗ Iℓ(˜ σ) is a ˜Eout ⊔ [ℓ]-deviation of ( gEnc out ⊗ ˜¯O) ⊗ Iℓ(˜ ρ). Corollary 7.18 reduces the alphabet size of a fault-tolerance scheme with just a mild decay in the fault-tolerance properties. However, the assumption that v′ ≥ v0(ϵ) means that we cannot apply Corollary 7.18 when the alphabet size is too small (i.e. below some constant assuming r, 1/ϵ =

O(1)). We therefore also show the more basic alphabet reduction result in Lemma 7.19 below, which works for arbitrary alphabet sizes, at the cost of a more severe decay in the fault-tolerance properties. This significant decay in parameters means that Lemma 7.19 will be unsuitable when the starting alphabet size is exponentially large in the number of physical qudits, in which case we will need to first apply Corollary 7.18. 110 Lemma 7.19. If Gt,i /∈ CF ∗ (so that the postselection ˜ζ are trivial as ˜Qt,i has no CF ∗ gates by Lemma 7.4): (a) If it holds for every ˜ t ∈ { T (t − 1) + 1 , . . . , T t } that supp( ˜F˜t) ∩ (N × Bt,i )| = ∅, (94) then for ˜ t ∈ { T (t − 1) + 1 , . . . , T t }, on qudits N × Bt,i ⊆ ˜N we let ˜F ′

gate in timestep T (t − 1) + 1, and then idles (i.e. applies identity gates) for the remaining timesteps T (t − 1) + 2 , . . . , T t . Therefore on qudits [ κ] × Bt,i , ˜Qt,i [ ˜F, ˜ζ] simply applies a CF ∗

gate followed by a Pauli error, given by the product (i.e. sequential composition) Ft,i of the restrictions of ˜FT (t−1)+1 , . . . , ˜FT t to qudits [ κ] × Bt,i . Meanwhile, on qudits N \ [κ] × Bt,i ,the circuit ˜Qt,i simply applies Init Z gates in timesteps T (t − 1) + 1 and T t , and idles for the intermediate timesteps T (t − 1) + 2 , . . . , T t − 1. Therefore on qudits N \ [κ] × Bt,i , ˜Qt,i [ ˜F, ˜ζ]has the effect of applying Init Z while rescaling the state by a phase βt,i (which may be 0) that arises when tracing out states given by applying Pauli superoperators to |0⟩ ⟨ 0| states. Thus we define ˜F ′ 

> T(t−1)+1

, . . . , ˜FT t −1 to idle on qudits N × Bt,i , and we let ˜FT t apply βt,i Ft,i 

to qudits [ κ] × Bt,i .We then have an equality of superoperators ˜Q[ ˜F′, ˜ζ]( ·) = ˜Q[ ˜F, ˜ζ]( ·), as ˜F′ implements the exact same Pauli corruption as ˜F, just sometimes in the roundabout fashions of: 1. swapping out some qudits in ˜N to ˜A, applying the corrupted gates on ˜A, and then swapping the qudits back, and           

> 30 In this expression ˜Qt,i [ ˜F,˜ζ], we implicitly restrict ˜F,˜ζto gates acting on qudits N×Bt,i , in timesteps T(t−1) + 1, . . . , T t .

113 2. accumulating Pauli errors across idling space-time regions of the circuit, and applying them together in the last timestep of the region. Specifically, each of the pairs of swaps described above is sandwiched by Init Z gates on the involved qudits in ˜A, and the postselection ˜ζ on the associated region in ˜N is trivial. Hence up to relabeling of some qudits from the swap gates, ˜Q[ ˜F′, ˜ζ]( ·) simply runs ˜Q[ ˜F, ˜ζ]( ·), while sometimes initializing an additional block of qudits in the |0⟩ state, applying Qt,i = QBt,i ,t (which is a channel due to the trivial postselection), and then tracing out this channel’s output. These additional initializations, channels, and tracing-outs have no effect on the execution of ˜Q[ ˜F, ˜ζ]( ·), as desired. Therefore it suffices to show that the resulting output ˜Q[ ˜F′, ˜ζ] ⊗ Iℓ(˜ σ) is a ˜Eout ⊔ [ℓ]-deviation of ( gEnc out ⊗ ˜¯O) ⊗ Iℓ(˜ ρ). 7.4 Main Result Proof 

In this section, we combine Theorem 6.1, Lemmas 7.2 and 7.19, and Corollary 7.18 to prove our main result Theorem 7.1, which we restate below for convenience. 

Theorem 7.1. For every prime power r, every 0 < ϵ < 1/8, and every ηgap ≥ 0, there exists 

¯N0 = ¯N0(r, ϵ ) ≥ 0 such that the following holds. For every set ¯N of size | ¯N | ≥ ¯N0 and every subset K ⊆ ¯N , there is a CSS non-subsystem code C(r, ϵ, K, ¯N ) with associated encoding map 

Enc C(r,ϵ,K, ¯N ), which satisfy the following for some integer M = M (r, ϵ, ¯N ) ≤ | ¯N |4+2 9ϵ:

n(r, ϵ, ¯N ) ≤ 232 √log | ¯N | · M · | ¯N |

d(r, ϵ, ¯N ) ≥ 2−29√log | ¯N | · M · | ¯N |.

2. For every CF -normalized 31 quantum circuit ¯Q = ( ¯Q1, . . . , ¯Q ¯T ; Kin , K out ) acting on a set of r-dimensional qudits labeled by ¯N using time ¯T and gate set G = {H∗, X∗, Z∗, CX ∗, CCX ∗, Init ∗, CF ∗},letting

and M satisfy the desired inequalities in Equations (115) and (116), while the codes C(r, ϵ, K, ¯N )have the desired parameters given by Equation (119). The circuit Q has the desired space and time usage in Equations (117) and (118). Thus the desired result holds.

# A Non-Subsystem Codes via Homological Products 

In this section, we show how to construct CSS non-subsystem codes with low-weight stabilizers by appropriately gauge-fixing the codes in Definition 4.1. While we will not directly use these non-subsystem codes in our fault-tolerance scheme, we believe them to be of independent interest. The main theorem of this section is stated below. 129 Theorem A.1. Let u, k, n, q 0, n ′

In Theorem A.1, if we for instance set k = (1 − 1/u )n, and for arbitrary constant ϵ > 0set u = ⌈1/2ϵ⌉, then letting n → ∞ , we obtain a family of explicit CSS non-subsystem codes with parameters [[ N, Θ( N ), Θ( N )]] q and stabilizer weight W ≤ N ϵ. Instead letting u grow slowly with n, such as u = log log log n, gives parameters [[ N, Θ( N ), ˜Ω( N )]] q and stabilizer weight W ≤

N 1−o(1) . These codes do require an exponentially large alphabet q = 2 ˜Θ( N ). See Section 2.1.5 for a comparison to prior code constructions. We now turn to proving Theorem A.1. We will use the following notion of homological product of quantum codes, which is similar to the subsystem product in Definition 3.8, but outputs a non-subsystem product code.

Single-sector homological products were first studied in the context of quantum codes by [BH14], and more recently by [ZP20, GG25b]. Similarly as in these prior works, we restrict attention to such products over fields of characteristic 2 to avoid signing issues. These products are a “single-sector” variant of the more standard tensor product (sometimes called a homological product) of chain complexes. These products were introduced for creating codes with low-weight stabilizers. Indeed, the stabilizer weight , meaning the maximum weight of any row or column of the matrix ∂

in Definition A.2, is at most P

ni.The homological product code in Definition A.2 has length n1 · · · nu; the following result of [BH14] shows that the dimension is k1 · · · ku, as for the subsystem product code in Defini-tion 3.8. While [BH14] only proved the u = 2 case of Proposition A.3 below, the result holds for general u ∈ N as stated below because the homological product over fields of characteristic 2 is by definition associative.

.131 Corollary A.4 was implicit in the proof of [GG25b, Theorem 4.1], which used a bound on subsystem product distance to bound homological product distance. We have included the general proof for completeness. In the language of stabilizer codes, Corollary A.4 can be interpreted as saying that the ho-mological product C is obtained from the subsystem product C′ by fixing the gauge qudits to an appropriate state. Applying this result with Theorem 4.2 immediately yields Theorem A.1: 

Proof of Theorem A.1. The result follows immediately by applying Corollary A.4 to the subsystem product codes in Theorem 4.2, instantiated with each ni = n, ki = k, and ℓi = ( n + k)/2. A similar result as in Theorem A.1 was proven in [GG25b, Corollary 4.7]. However, in The-orem A.1 we use explicit Reed-Solomon codes from Theorem 4.2, whereas the construction of [GG25b] was non-explicit, and in fact used uniformly random codes. Whereas our distance proof in Theorem 4.2 (which implies that in Theorem A.1) is a fairly concise algebraic argument, [GG25b] bounded distance using the notion of higher-order product-expansion , which was shown in [KP25] to be satisfied by random codes. 132
